citizens with this purpose. we believe in our group that the road map should be an ambitious one, a progressive one. it means that if we start with the bratislava road map, which pointed out the need for strengthening european defence and security, we can agree with it but also say clearly that it is not enough. we need to have a road map where social europe is really at the heart to reconnect with citizens, making the best of the upcoming european pillar of social rights, and we need also to make sure that the growth and jobs strategy will be underpinned by a more powerful investment plan, aligned with sustainable development goals, and these goals should also inspire our development policy because this is the only way to go to the source of the oncoming migration wave. we need to mr president, i would naturally like to greet president antonio tajani and to wish him the very best of luck and i am sure that we will have two and a half years of an exemplary presidency. as regards the maltese presidency of the council, what i wanted to make clear here is essentially this: the stakes in resolving the refugee and migration crisis are very high and malta has many lessons to teach all the countries of the european union because it is facing the problem with a single vision because of its geographical situation. i regret that, despite being very focused on security, you have not spoken about common defence policy, a point which i think is essential for the next six months and let me now finally just make a point to mrs dalli to tell her that it is regrettable what she has said here about mr david casa, who defends the interests of malta with all his might and who is right because in this house there is a lot of concern about the suspicions of corruption that are going on around the maltese issue and so we are paying attention to the panama papers, we are paying attention to the issues of corruption in malta, now to say that mr casa is not a patriot, that is totally unacceptable and it is regrettable in a debate of this nature.
the programme of the maltese presidency, in the wake of its predecessors, highlights the deep and persistent crisis in the european union, which is an expression of the more general crisis of capitalism and which is simultaneously a crisis of the pillars and foundations of the eu itself, as the process of capitalist integration it is. it also highlights the lack of solutions to the problems affecting the peoples of europe - unemployment, poverty, exclusion, social inequalities and injustices, precariousness in work and life. this programme is also a cause for serious concern, given the clear prospect of further steps in the military and security escalation the eu has embarked upon. the reactionary, backward, inhuman and dangerous nature of the eu is being accentuated. this is evident from the outset in immigration policies. the aim is to impose the generalization of the eu-turkey agreement to all african countries of origin and transit of migrants, with the aim of stopping and/or expelling all those fleeing war and hunger - for which the eu has known responsibilities - who risk their lives to reach european soil. the brexit process, which is still marked by great disorientation, is being driven by interests that are contrary to the interests of the people, and which are an expression of the contradictions between powers and fractions of capital that mark the current stage of development of capitalism and the eu.
the programme of the maltese presidency does not address the continuing crisis in the european union, which is an expression of the more general crisis of capitalism and which is at the same time a crisis of the pillars and foundations of the eu itself as a process of capitalist integration. it confirms the exhaustion and lack of solutions to the problems affecting the peoples of europe: unemployment, poverty, exclusion, social inequalities and injustices, precariousness in work and life. this lack of solutions is a kind of escape, highlighting the worst aspects of european policies, particularly in the areas of migration, financial policies and the fight against tax fraud and money laundering, except that malta is one of the eu's most active offshore assets.
the reasons for the agony of the european union lie more on this side of the atlantic than on the other. in the first fortnight of this year the huge mass grave in the mediterranean has claimed over 200 lives, official figures, some reports point to over 400 dead. the almost three thousand migrants who have arrived on european soil in recent days are facing the inhumanity of the european union: detention camps where they are left to freeze to death before being deported. terrorism, under the guise of fighting terrorism. instead of guaranteeing people the security to enjoy their rights, they restrict and attack rights for alleged security reasons. in the end, no rights, no security. this european council is a clear indication of the impasse in which the european union finds itself. the euro accentuates its role as a ruthless engine of divergence and impoverishment for the periphery. in the netherlands, there is another referendum that has been disregarded, this one under the association agreement with ukraine, because, amidst the rubble of this exhausted integration process, part of the problems and not the solution will be left to the peoples in their struggle to open a new path of progress and cooperation in europe.
madam president, citizens increasingly see the european councils as a technocratic formality from which they do not expect any break or consequential decisions capable of improving everyday life and strengthening confidence in the european project. and this council we are discussing here has not escaped the rule. on migration, there were positive steps in external partnerships, in particular the progress of the european fund for sustainable development, but there was no internal partnership for investment, growth and employment. in the field of security, there was a positive will to strengthen the strengthened corporations, in particular the creation of the european defence fund, but there was no concrete plan for cooperation in research, technological development and the joint and shared management of strategic data. in the developmental plane, the exhaustive repetition of good ideas and intentions. however, it is necessary to add robust funding models that allow for the passage, and this is the essential question, of intentions to acts. the rome summit, which marks the 60th anniversary of the treaty which laid the foundations of the european union, cannot be a replica of these councils, full of words but with no consequences. the european union needs to respond clearly to the new global challenges. yesterday, here in parliament, a choice was made which, while democratic and legitimate, was anything but clear. skeptics, europeans and even euro-enthusiasts have negotiated a solution which paves the way for the european union to continue to sail on sight and so we cannot continue in this process, we have to resist this temptation. those who resist will be on the right side of history.
the financial transaction tax aims to bring fairness and regulation to tax systems in europe and to ensure a more than adequate elementary contribution from the financial sector to the budgets of the member states and the union, especially as austerity policies brutally tax the middle classes and small and medium-sized enterprises. this tax must, of course, have a global impact. and that is why i regret that the maltese presidency has not made tax harmonization, transparency and the fight against money laundering among its priorities, especially when members of its government are implicated in the panama papers and this is also a vital issue in the fight against organised crime, including terrorism. malta, one of the countries which the commission and the council have tolerated as specialising in tax competition and aggressive and opaque practices, now has special responsibilities, increased responsibilities, as the presidency of the european union, to take forward the measures already proposed by the commission to combat these practices, including unblocking in the council the enhanced cooperation project on the financial transaction tax, which has already been initiated by ten member states, including my own, portugal. (the speaker accepts the answer to a question on the 'blue card' (rule 162 (8)).
blue-card answer. – the ftt is also to have a redistributive impact, and that is also essential to control a financial system that has gone totally out of control, as we saw in the financial crisis. if we want to start to control the financial system, we absolutely need to have this tax paid. it is also a way to contribute to our national budgets, as well as to the budget of the eu, and is a crucial question for social justice, without which citizens will not trust our governments.
in writing. beyond the horrors of war, terrorism and sectarian violence they have escaped, beyond the thousands of deaths in the mediterranean, beyond the long and arduous journeys by land, beyond the closed borders, the misunderstanding and hostility they have in many cases faced, refugees now face the rigors of winter, which this year has brought europe adverse weather conditions not seen in a long time. there are tens of thousands of refugees in overcrowded camps, often made up of unheated, untied, and precarious tents. it is clear that this drama also stems from the exasperatingly slow process of redistributing refugees among the member states, which has so far involved only a few thousand people. europe has a humanitarian and moral obligation to use all the instruments and resources to help these refugees, including babies, children and the elderly. we therefore welcome the series of decisions taken by the commission, in conjunction with the national authorities, to create suitable shelters and improve existing ones, to distribute heating and to move people in particularly vulnerable situations to suitable places.
rapporteur. – mr president, the european parliament will adopt today the most important political initiative for developing a social dimension during this legislature, and for giving citizens the means to get control of their lives and restoring trust in the european union project. the european parliament wants this to be a central priority in the roadmap for the future of the european union to be adopted at the next summit in rome on 25 march. we must put the social dimension at the heart of this roadmap for the future of the european union. this european parliament report is based on very long work by our members, and also involving many other stakeholders across europe who were extremely active in building up a new vision for the social dimension of the european union. so first of all i would like to thank all of you. now is the moment, once the report is adopted, for the commission to come also with a bold proposal, to put it on the table at the council of ministers, and finally on the table of the european council. the european parliament will stand ready to set this deal with the commission, the council, and the european council. we must have a vision of a social dimension able to cope with the current challenges, which are not only global competition and ageing trends, but also a very important digital revolution, and internal problems and internal divergences in the eurozone. this report must become a turning point when updating social standards and security and fundamental social rights for all european citizens. this pillar should address the entire european union. first of all, by giving children a real guarantee to avoid poverty; delivering a strong youth guarantee; and organising the transition between education and employment in such a way that we make the best of the youngest generation in europe. we need also to make sure that all workers, whatever their jobs, can count on a clear contract with social rights, decent working conditions and full access to social protection; and we want all citizens to have a european social security card and a personal account where they will build their rights to social protection. the skills guarantee must become a new social right for the 21st century. finally, we must ensure that women and men can reconcile their working life with family life, using not only childcare services but all the leave schemes we need to legislate the plan. we want poverty to be tackled with more effective minimum—income schemes all over europe. these are key issues for this pillar, but we want the means to deliver, and the means are labour legislation, changing the way the european semester works, and addressing the financial means we need to have to implement all these social standards.
mr president, the attack on social rights in europe has been promoted for decades by the right and social democracy and stems from your class matrix. are you concerned about the social deficit in europe? they advocate the regulation of working hours, the working day not exceeding 8 hours. equal pay for equal work, the end of all forms of precarious work, collective bargaining and the role of workers' organisations, real wage increases and a fair and effective distribution of wealth, universal and solidarity-based public social security, proposals which are not in this report. this social pillar opens the way to harmonization of employment, to harmonization of social protection and security systems. it will be another achievement that will continue the offensive against workers' rights, reduce the standards of labour and social rights subject to the mechanisms of economic governance and the euro, fuelling the policies of impoverishment and exploitation. we cannot do a social washing of an anti-social building and expect progress to be made.
madam president, dear colleague maria joão rodrigues, there are many points of convergence between the epp and the socialists on the european project. it's been that way throughout the history of this institution. we agree that economic policies must have a positive social effect, that there must be adequate funding for european policies and programmes. we agree that economic convergence has not been accompanied by the ambitious social convergence. the central question is what mechanisms must be put in place to ensure that there is also social harmonization, thus fulfilling the european project. but there are points that separate us, congresswoman. in order to contribute to the development of europe, we must take account of how it works, of the powers of the commission and of the powers of the member states, because if we do not do this we are making empty promises to our fellow citizens. i do not want to be counted on to make demagogic proposals which are so arbitrary that they are empty, or which call into question the sovereignty of the member states or which disrespect the social partners. while you have been holding press conferences in the last few hours, accusing the epp of distorting your report, we have been negotiating so that we can approve a european social pillar shortly. without the epp amendments this would not have been possible. we did so because we are not motivated by partisan tricks, but by defending the interests of the european citizens.
the so-called 'european pillar of social rights', as presented here by rapporteur maria joão rodrigues, should be seen as the cornerstone of european cohesion. among these rights, of course, the right to a european minimum wage stands out, that is to say, a threshold below which it is not permitted to pay for work. social rights are today one of the fundamental dimensions of human rights and these, taken as a whole, can only be realised historically if people's basic needs are met. in this context, therefore, the fight against poverty is now of strategic importance not only because of its effects on economic growth but also as a major factor in social peace. poverty always ends up breeding violence.
rapporteur. – mr president, today’s debate was very important because it was made clear for everybody that, with this report, parliament sees what is at stake, which is making sure that there will be a turning point in the direction in which the european project is going. without a stronger social dimension, there is no future for the european union. this came out of this debate very clearly. i believe that with this report we can lay down the conditions for a new stage of european social policy because what is at stake is no longer only making a level playing field for the single market to operate. we do need this, but it is not enough. what is at stake is also being able to cope with common challenges, notably the upcoming digital revolution which will profoundly change the way in which the markets operate. what is at stake is to organise a full process of upward economic and social convergence in the european union and inside the eurozone. in order to do this we need to combine powerful means: labour and social law, changing the direction of economic governance, and providing the necessary financial means. regarding the so—called european semester, we need to have stronger social targets along with economic targets, focusing on the problems of unemployment, social inequalities and poverty to make sure that economic policy takes into account the social impact. we need to have a new kind of interaction between economic and social policy. secondly, we need to have the appropriate means, starting with the means for social investment in education, training, tackling poverty and providing good social services. this should also be translated into the community budget, the european fund for strategic investment, as well as a proper fiscal capacity in the eurozone. these ideas are included in our report and, through these, we also want to establish positive cooperation between our colleagues in the committee on economic and monetary affairs and those in the committee on employment and social affairs. this is the kind of cooperation we need to develop in order to implement such a pillar. i would like to thank all the shadows once again. our citizens are making a clear call for a strong social union. now we just need to deliver. (applause)

mr vice-president, i welcome the presentation of the report on the progress of renewable energy in the framework of the energy union. this progress is more important today than ever, because with the beginning of the new american administration a new matrix in the political and economic relationship on a global scale has also begun. and in that matrix, energy, for its role in economic competitiveness and the well-being of populations and its impact on the sustainability of the planet, is and will be at the center of geopolitics and geoeconomics for years to come. the european union has been a leader in the energy transition and must continue to be so, combining efficiency, security, market integration, research, innovation and investment in infrastructure. and renewable energy is the focus of differentiation. but progress is not enough: 12% of total energy and 28.3% of electricity consumed is very little. we must act faster, review the renewable energy directive, invest in decarbonising transport and invest in new interconnections. here are three examples of what we need to do to go faster.
this single energy market is an agenda that is inseparable from the objectives of the major european groups in the sector and the powers that defend their interests. in essence, it advocates the liberalisation of the 'energy market', pressing member states to accelerate the process of privatising what is still public. without recognizing that, contrary to miraculous promises, neither workers nor consumers have benefited from liberalization. energy is a strategic sector, fundamental to the sovereign economic development of the country, supported by scientific knowledge, technological progress and environmental sustainability. reason enough for it to be kept in the public domain, under democratic scrutiny and control. this report is incompatible with those principles. and this is our position, and reality is gradually proving us right!
five thousand deaths in the mediterranean in 2016 and tens of thousands of refugees in deplorable conditions in greece and bulgaria tragically demonstrate that europe has not been able to manage migration flows along mediterranean or other routes, has no common asylum system that works effectively and continues to fuel the business of trafficking networks by not opening legal and safe pathways for immigrant refugees, including through humanitarian visas. the malta summit will be a failure and a disgrace if it is based on outsourcing our responsibilities and our borders. supporting the training of libyan structures of the unhcr and the mfa to rescue and ensure the dignified treatment of migrants and libyan refugees, yes, mrs mogherini, but not to impose readmission agreements or replicas of the illegal trade with turkey on a ungoverned libya. it would be indecent and counterproductive. it is imperative that our governments come together, reinvigorate european solidarity to respond to those from inside and outside, like trump and his strategist putin, who want to destroy the european union because they want to destroy democracy and, to do this, ride the xenophobic, racist, nationalist forces, who want to prevent us from fulfilling our basic obligations regarding refugees and migrants.
the commission is also aware of the fact that the south sudanese government has not yet taken action to address the problem of the sudanese refugees. in their strategy of dominating peoples and plundering their wealth, the great powers divide states, foment wars and, if necessary, intervene militarily, as in libya or mali. we cannot disconnect the current situation from the process favored and promoted by the united states, the european union, israel and their allies, exploiting and exacerbating internal differences, poverty, social injustices and ethnic differences, to fuel a protracted conflict that would lead to their independence in 2011, in a process very similar to kosovo. all in the name of geostrategic control and exploitation of the considerable oil reserves and various minerals in the southern region, which still today are the driving force behind international interests in the region. we need a call for a ceasefire and an immediate end to the financing and sale of arms to the various parties to the conflict. it is necessary to defend a peaceful and political solution to the conflict, free from any external military interference and intervention.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in 2015, in kenya, barack obama said the following: there is no excuse for sexual assault or domestic violence. there is no reason for young girls to undergo genital mutilation. these traditions may be centuries old, barack obama said, but they have no place in the 21st century. next february 6th, we mark yet another international day of zero tolerance for female genital mutilation and the problem persists. the european union has now reiterated its commitment to eliminating this practice one year ago. this hidden violence has nothing to do with culture. it's pure torture. the international community must commit itself to the criminalisation of this practice in all countries. a man who mistreats his wife and children cannot be a credible partner. i thank the commissioner for pointing out what has been done, but i have some specific questions i would like to have answered. what are the results in the communities that do this? what is the situation with data collection and processing inside and outside the european union? and what punitive measures are being envisaged? finally, what strength do we have in terms of foreign policy to combat precisely this scourge?
in writing. the next 6 february will once again be the international day for the elimination of female genital mutilation. it is estimated that at least 200 million girls and women have been subjected to this practice, in clear violation of their fundamental rights. in the european union alone, as of 2013, 180 000 girls are still at risk of female genital mutilation every year. since 2013, some steps have been taken. the european parliament has recently called for the ratification of the istanbul convention. but much remains to be done: adequate international protection policies, information on national policies, more regular statistics. it is also important to assess the state of implementation of the combat action plan. that is why parliament must once again mark this date and call for a greater effort.
mr president, the amendment to the regulation we are debating today focuses mainly on bananas, which are one of the most widely consumed fruits in the european union, accounting for a third of world exports, the main source being ecuador. european producers are concentrated in the outermost regions of portugal, spain and france and account for only 11.4% of european consumption, which is their only market. in portugal, production is located mainly in the autonomous region of madeira and in the azores, and it is an important lever for economic development, job creation, population consolidation and, interestingly, also for the landscape modelling that must be preserved. i would like to stress the importance of this interinstitutional commitment to the creation of reliable statistical instruments on the development of import quantities and prices, the assessment of the situation of european producers and the submission of a report on the application of the stabilisation mechanism for bananas. i would also stress the establishment of a system of warning about the effects of imports on the european market and the adoption of possible compensatory measures. finally, i would stress the possibility and the interest in extending the validity of these mechanisms beyond 2020, following what is proposed by the rapporteur herself.
economic and trade relations between the european union and the countries of latin america are of the utmost importance and i believe that in the present international circumstances they must be rapidly increased. ecuador and colombia, which produce more than half of the bananas consumed in europe, are two examples of countries which deserve help and support from the european union. but on the other hand, we must not forget that there are 37 000 jobs directly or indirectly dependent on banana production in five countries of the european union. my country, portugal, is the third largest producer of bananas in europe. madeira and the azores produce some 20 000 tonnes of this fruit each year. this sector must be cautious, particularly because this activity concerns the outermost territories, islands which are geographically quite isolated and, therefore, less able to diversify their economy in the event of serious losses. the safeguard mechanisms now established by the european commission and now fine-tuned by this parliament, with a view to better monitoring and control, are a valuable contribution to correcting imbalances in the european market resulting from substantial increases in banana imports. they prove that the expectations and concerns of the producers of this fruit, most of whom are small producers, are not unimportant to us in the european context. the solution found has the merit of safeguarding european interests without jeopardizing, as we believe is equally important, the interests of those latin american countries whose banana export volumes will continue to have room for growth.
mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner and the other members who have worked so hard on this agreement that i hope that today and tomorrow we will have a very positive vote and that this agreement will be approved. an agreement that safeguards all european banana farmers and producers. the biggest victory in terms of negotiations has been achieved. from now on, we will have a safeguard clause that may suspend imports from central america. from now on, we will have access to more information on imported quantities. the impact of imports on local economies will now be analysed. and what impact do these imports have on regions like ours, the outermost regions? the banana producers in the azores and especially in madeira are effectively protected by this new regulation. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, commissioner, first of all i must congratulate the rapporteur on the inclusive work she has done with the shadow rapporteurs and their teams. we may disagree on some details, but everything was done with openness, clarity and commitment on the part of all the political groups involved in the discussion of a regulation which is of enormous relevance to the management of external fisheries. since time is short, i want to focus on three key points. firstly, i think it is essential that this regulation should not be stripped of a provision which already existed before and which allows for the penalisation of vessels and masters who have committed serious infringements for access to international fisheries agreements. it would be a historic step backwards in the context of european legislation. secondly, i think it is essential to ensure that there is transparency in the information on vessels fishing in international waters, what fishing opportunities are associated with them, who are their owners and, very importantly, who are the final beneficiaries, that is, who are the beneficial owners. we must ensure that european fisheries are not used as a tool for tax evasion or money laundering. finally, i should like to explain why i think it is important to prevent the perpetuation of dormant international agreements. in ensuring the legality and transparency of the sector, which is guaranteed by this regulation, we must also ensure that we do not perpetuate dormant agreements to the detriment of the interests of the fleets of the member states of the european union, with a harmful effect downstream and, while we are asleep, promote the establishment of dubious and malicious fleets. if we want to combat iuu fishing and slavery and promote respect for the environment and local communities in third countries, then we cannot allow inefficient european international agreements to continue for at least three more years.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, this debate calls our attention to one of the most serious problems in the european union today: the promiscuity between the political and the judicial authorities, between the political and the judicial authorities. the democratic rule of law is only democratic if it is based on the rule of law and it is only based on the rule of law if it is democratic. this separation of powers implies that judicial investigation is not done according to political opportunity, it means that politics does not take advantage of judicial investigations. politics cannot be judicialized, just as justice cannot be politicized. the rule of law is a state which is based on the law in its concrete actions and the law is dictated by the courts, not by the secret services or any other institutions of a political or administrative nature. criminal investigation cannot be used for political purposes, just as criminal investigation cannot be used for political purposes. you cannot manipulate the judiciary, nor can you manipulate the political power.
the visa-free regime for entering georgia and the european union is desirable and appropriate, as georgia has taken all necessary measures to meet the standards of the european union for more than a year. and its people and institutions are committed to consolidating democracy and imbued with a pro-european spirit, as i saw as head of the european parliament's election observation mission in georgia a few months ago. it is all the more important that this regime comes into force without delay in the face of the tectonic changes taking place in the international political context, with trump, president of the united states, serving putin's strategy to destroy the european union and democracy. we must therefore reorganise our forces and support all those in georgia who oppose the russian occupation of their territory and who are working to strengthen economic and political relations with the european union. the visa waiver will facilitate the exchange of people at all levels and will give the democratic forces in georgia a clear signal that we want to help them consolidate their democracy and their autonomy. i would like to thank the president of the georgian parliament and his delegation who are here today, and my colleague maria gabriel for her commitment as rapporteur in this process.

madam president, the last issue of portuguese ten-year bonds was at interest rates above 4%. the threats from the eurogroup are back. it is demonstrated that it is impossible to defend portugal's interests without addressing the question of the debt and the euro. it is illusory to expect that the deficit settlement or subordination to the debt burden will result in some kind of mercy on the part of the markets and their political representatives. if we exclude interest on the debt, portugal has a primary budget balance of over eur 4 billion. lacking monetary sovereignty, the euro periphery is totally dependent on the choices of the ecb, which it does not control, or on the blackmail of the rating agencies. in other words, the euro has drawn the line between the troika's blackmail and the blackmail of the financial markets. the solution is not to give in to any of them, but to recover monetary sovereignty and to withdraw from the euro those member states which consider its continued existence unsustainable, and to renegotiate the debt, the terms, interest and amounts, preferably within the framework negotiated at european level.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i have repeatedly confronted this house, the european commission and the portuguese government with the daily risks posed to portugal by the almaraz nuclear power station, a few kilometres from portugal and the tagus river. almaraz is an obsolete power plant, a real time bomb that should have been closed in 2010, but which has seen its life extended until 2020. the portuguese, 40 years ago in ferrel, were massively opposed to the construction of a nuclear power station on their territory, as well as to the nuclear projects that spain has been carrying out on our territory, in violation of the international obligations to which it is bound. the commission notes that the spanish authorities have not provided any evidence that the spanish authorities have not taken the necessary measures to ensure that the waste from the nuclear power plant in almaraz is not recycled. it also shows the will to extend the operation of almaraz to 60 years of life, as happened last week with the decision to reopen the garoña nuclear power station, which is celebrating its 60th anniversary. ladies and gentlemen, we cannot allow the portuguese to live in constant fear. portugal cannot become spain's nuclear waste dump.

on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, the european union must define an ambitious roadmap for the future. the european parliament wants this to happen at the rome summit and, indeed, in the white paper that is to be presented by the commission. we should not wait for upcoming national elections. the european union is being challenged right now by the combined effect of putin, trump and internal nationalistic voices, and we believe that this is the time for the european union to assert itself as what it is already: a powerful democratic political and economic entity. we need to translate this into a roadmap with better european solutions: better european solutions for growth with an investment strategy, a strong social pillar and completing economic and monetary union with a proper fiscal capacity; better european solutions to our security problems, certainly by advancing european defence and internal security, and also by ensuring a proper european asylum system and the proper management of migration, in cooperation with the european neighbourhood. but the european union should also send a message to the world, saying that we, the european union, are in favour of openness and cooperation based on a properly regulated global economy. the world is waiting for this positive message from the european union. then we need to translate all this into new means of taking action. that has implications for the community budget. we need a community budget that is turned to the future. we believe that this offers the best way for citizens to take control of their lives, and that the best way to assure national sovereignty is to strengthen european sovereignty.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the three reports we have discussed here on the future of europe are a very clear message from this european parliament in favour of a stronger, more united and more democratic europe. and let us not be under any illusions, the task before us is to save the european project, nothing less. this objective is vital for peace, for the quality of life of citizens, for the regulation of globalisation. it is a possible goal if the european project is reconciled with its values, if solidarity prevails over national egotism and if europe resumes its lost ambition for prosperity and convergence. there are of course no magic solutions, but to meet the aspirations of its citizens, the european union needs a new impetus for prosperity and convergence. and what these three reports tell us is that there will be neither prosperity nor convergence, if we do not learn the lessons of the financial crisis, if we do not correct the mistakes in the architecture of the euro and if we do not really revive that ambition at the heart of the european project. that is the task before us, and it is what we must achieve if there is political will.

on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, the epp welcomes the growth in all the member states of the european union and the decline in unemployment, particularly among young people. these being the objectives set by the european semester process, we can be pleased with the results we are achieving. however, this satisfaction cannot give way to any accommodation. in addition to continuing to pursue these objectives, we must develop ambitious policies that translate into greater social cohesion and strengthen the economic sustainability of the european union and its member states, enhancing our autonomy in the face of possible external shocks that may arise. this ambition must be achieved by investing in human capital as a means of boosting productivity, matching the skills of workers to the needs of enterprises and ensuring the social integration of the most disadvantaged. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. it also requires an individual approach to the unemployed at member state level, in particular the long-term unemployed, ensuring adequate support in terms of training and reintegration mechanisms, and working to ensure that they do not lead to processes of exploiting cheap labour. it also implies the definition of policies based on the virtuous triangle of investment, which brings economic and social returns, the definition of structural reforms that ensure the sustainability of the european economic and social model and fiscal consolidation, ensuring a decent future for future generations. as tempting as it is to focus on current results, we must be able to look ahead. as much as we are politically tempted to give momentary positive answers, we have a duty to guarantee the model of social development which characterizes the european union and its member states.
on behalf of the s&d group, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr gunnar hökmark, for the way in which we have cooperated in the preparation of his report, which is a good compromise between the different political positions in this parliament. the central issue is clear: the monetary policy of the european central bank is not enough to ensure sustained economic growth and employment, nor to promote the new impetus for prosperity and convergence that europe needs. in this report, therefore, parliament supports a strategy which, not just in words but in reality, is based on a virtuous triangle of public and private investment, in particular in infrastructure, in socially balanced structural reforms respecting social rights and in responsible public finances, with full respect for the flexibility allowed by the rules. but there is one more point to be made: the proposal, the commission's great novelty, is the defence of a moderately expansionary budgetary policy. this is not a small thing after years of austerity. the socialist group supports this commission proposal and we are pleased that parliament will state in its report that this is an important development, which allows for the coordination of economic policies and which points to the opportunities for the states with budgetary leeway to promote budgetary stimulus, to grow domestic demand and the european economy. there will be no lack of support for the commission in this parliament for a more expansionary budgetary policy.
the european semester is once again confirming its nature as another cycle of austerity and neoliberal policies. twenty-five years after the adoption of the maastricht treaty, it is now all too evident that the euro and its instruments of economic governance have not delivered on the promises of stability, economic growth and cohesion. the euro is now recognised as an instrument of divergence which has dismantled much of the economies of the member states, imposing cuts in investment, privatisations, attacks on the social functions of the state and its public services. fifteen years after the introduction of the euro, portugal is poorer and more dependent, has deviated from the european union average, and is struggling with the burden of a debt imposed on it by the troika and the policies of the euro and the european union. in the face of this situation, in the face of the deep discontent of the population and of the workers, these reports point to more of the same: the same infernal triangle of austerity that binds the national states and prevents, with the threats of the budgetary treaty, any alternative policy. the euro is now part of the problem, not the solution. in this sense, we believe that the liberation of portugal from the constraints of the euro is a necessary and indispensable condition for putting our country back on the path of development.
madam president, commissioner, the trade agreement with canada is of strategic importance for the european union, especially after trump, and is the necessary response against the dangerous illusion of isolation and protectionism. this is also a good agreement, an economically balanced agreement, which clarifies tariffs and allows market access, and is a new benchmark for international trade, a progressive agreement for a more regulated globalisation. today we can say that we listened to the citizens and that this agreement, under pressure from this parliament and civil society, respects the environmental, social and human values of the european union, our right to regulate in the public interest. it was under pressure from this parliament and the socialist group that the private arbitration system was replaced by a new public arbitration system and it is because this agreement has been improved that its final version deserves the confidence of the citizens and deserves the favourable vote of this european parliament.
mr president, commissioner, this agreement, which was launched in 2009 and concluded in 2014, is, in our opinion, quite comprehensive, modern, transparent and also forward-looking. we believe that it will now bring benefits to businesses, workers and consumers. the agreement, which eliminates 98% of customs duties, also provides for the safeguarding of public services, audiovisual services, transport and the most sensitive agricultural products. the agreement will guarantee the protection of 143 european geographical indications, 20 of which are portuguese. we also believe that mutual recognition of certification for many products is important, as is recognition of a wide range of professions, from engineers to architects to lawyers. we also want to stress the need to maintain high standards in the areas of the environment, food safety, health and consumer rights, while respecting compliance with european rules and regulations. i am sure that the european union will benefit from this agreement and my country will not be an exception, as portugal currently has over a thousand companies exporting to canada, 89% of them being small and mediumsized enterprises which provide 12 000 jobs. that is why i am voting in favour of ceta.
mr. chairman, i voted for this free trade agreement. i am very pleased to have been present at this historic vote and i am convinced that this agreement will lead to a strengthening of trade relations between the european union and canada. i am, after all, in favour of the principles of free trade in general, and i am opposed to tariffs and other trade barriers, which we as the european union should oppose and fight. the european union, one of the ideas of our integration, is the free movement of goods, services, capital and people, and this agreement fits into our philosophy, our cooperation of free states under the rule of law. i am therefore very pleased that this agreement has been adopted and i hope that it will meet the expectations that it will lead to an increase in trade between the european union and canada of up to 20%, and that the benefits will be in the billions of euros for both sides and that it will lead to more jobs being created, not to what the left is afraid of, namely fewer jobs.
mr president, i voted in favour of the conclusion of a comprehensive economic and trade agreement between canada and the european union in recognition that canada is an important trade and investment partner and ally for the european union and that the agreement is an important instrument in the european union's efforts to set a rigorous framework for world trade rules. in my decision i took account of the fact that we are living in a time of uncertainty and ambiguity and a resurgence of dangerous protectionist policies, which are a setback for the multilateral and bilateral trading system and open up uncertainty about the future of trade relations. i therefore believe that the european union should therefore strengthen partnerships and closer cooperation models with states with which it shares common values and strategic interests. however, i would like to stress that in environmental matters ceta should have been more ambitious and more in line with the commitments made in paris and even with the european project itself in terms of climate energy objectives, which aim at a green, sustainable economy, giving priority to renewable energy and the fight against climate change. ceta should also promote sustainable agricultural practices more rigorously, as the link between energy, food and water is essential for citizens' well-being, poverty reduction and sustainable development.
mr president, this agreement between the parties was a very, very partial agreement. on the one hand, we have three and a half million people who have signed, who have sent us their signatures, in fact, rejecting this agreement. we also have two thousand other regions that have declared themselves free from ceta and ttip, and on the other side we have the multinationals and profit. the majority of this parliament has decided to choose multinationals and profit over those who are the interests of citizens. besides, he had a behavior, i would say, strange. it is a parliament which votes in favour of an agreement by a majority, but which has no political position on it. it is not capable of making a political resolution which can set out the position of parliament. so it is a parliament that does not think. i would therefore like to say that the citizens will continue to resist and that the national parliaments will certainly have to listen to these citizens. the game is not over yet and i am certainly on the side of the people, not the side of profit.
this directive, which was triggered by the november 2015 terrorist attacks in paris, aims to harmonise criminal law, improve the exchange of information and prevent further terrorist attacks. parliament has succeeded in improving a number of provisions, warning of the danger of over-reacting to a situation that is not part of a comprehensive strategy to combat radicalisation. the directive has three main problems. first, criminal offences are not adequately defined, leaving the door open to interpretations that may disproportionately restrict fundamental rights. the fight against terrorism on platforms such as the internet must be done in a smart and technically effective way, which is very different from criminalizing the use of the internet itself. it is also important that the commission, which promises better legislation, has once again dispensed with the impact assessment. third, the foreign fighters terminology is wrong. the overwhelming majority of terrorists who have attacked in europe are europeans, disintegrated but born and raised here; they did not need to travel abroad. fundamental to combating terrorism is not in this directive or any other. it demands that governments, our governments, abandon authoritarian neoliberal policies, that cut off resources to equip and train police and law enforcement, that create ghettos and segregation in our cities, that turn prisons into centers of radicalization instead of rehabilitation. and those same governments must stop the importation of wahabi fundamentalist propagandists into european mosques. (the president agreed to answer a question under the 'blue card' procedure, pursuant to rule 162 (8) of the rules of procedure)
blue-card answer. – i know that several anti-democratic, autocratic governments are using the excuse of terrorism, and the obsession with the fight against terrorism, to actually suppress basic freedoms and suppress legitimate criticism and dissent. we cannot align ourselves with that and that is also why we need to be very critical about what is happening inside the eu and that is why i am also critical about some of the solutions that are in this directive that, in my view, put in question disproportionately, or may put in question disproportionately, the fundamental rights of the european citizens themselves.
in writing. the storyline is not new. it is necessary to 'respond' to attacks on european soil and to 'fight' so-called 'foreign fighters'. the european union has not yet been able to put forward a comprehensive strategy for combating terrorism. the legislative proposal aims at criminalising 'activities related to terrorist practices'. the absence of a definition of 'terrorism', and the wide and narrow scope of measures and legal persons liable to be charged proposed, will lead to the generalization of criminalisation for 'practices linked to terrorism', with questionable causality. the mere fact of visiting a website, or of travelling to certain regions, could constitute a legal basis for detention and criminalization. the member states will have the freedom to close websites without the need for judicial authorisation. at a time when the eu institutions are talking so much about so-called 'radicalisation', they are helping to tighten the rope of marginalisation and exclusion, creating stigmas and feelings of rejection on both sides. this is a dangerous legislative initiative which continues the eu's security drift, consolidating a strong europe that makes a clean slate of the most basic rights and freedoms, imposing control, registration, profiling, censorship.
madam president, mr president of the council, mr commissioner king, dear colleague monica macovei, the introduction of tighter controls through systematic consultation of existing european and national databases is a good example of how we can give immediate security to european citizens. to ensure that the schengen area remains an area of freedom, security and justice, we must act at the external borders and ensure the best possible cooperation internally. it is therefore crucial that we can ensure full control of who is crossing our external border, as these amendments seek to do - not just for those who enter but also for those who leave, not just for third country nationals but also for europeans. we want to optimise the use of information systems such as sis, the schengen information system, in order to enhance information sharing and cooperation between member states to make these amendments to the schengen borders code as effective as possible. that is why the proposal put forward by the commission last december to amend the sis cannot be dissociated from these measures. and that concludes my remarks, madam president. the security of europeans cannot be guaranteed by isolated layers; the security of europeans is achieved in a planned way, by ensuring a balanced approach, because it is our duty to ensure security, but also freedom, including freedom of movement for all europeans.
in writing. information sharing is a key pillar of the union's internal security strategy. it is unfortunately commonplace to acknowledge that cooperation between member states in this field is still far from perfect. however, the union is possibly undergoing one of the most intensive re-designations of its information systems, with changes to sis, vis and eurodac, alongside the creation of the new entry/exit system and etias. in parallel, although ideally upstream, the high level group on information systems and interoperability will publish the outcome of its work in may this year. it is clear that the prum decisions and the council decision on the sharing of information in the field of terrorism must be adapted to the new challenges and the new legal framework of the lisbon treaty. however, caution is advised. any changes should be made taking into account the entire information systems landscape the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial instruments are effective, proportionate and necessary. otherwise, we are not protecting our citizens.
madam president, we have identified several gaps in the report. on the one hand, it does not advocate that the development of these areas be subordinated to national interests, supported by public policies of investment, research and development. at the same time, it takes a very narrow approach to the impacts on the world of work, namely the increase in precariousness, impoverishment, unemployment and the liquidation of labour and social rights, omitting the interests of capital in its development, in order to increase exploitation with a view to maximising profit. the absence of any reference to and condemnation of the relationship between robotics and its use for military and security purposes is also incomprehensible. we have moved away from the approach of legislative harmonization in this area and the creation of another agency which contributes to conditioning and limiting the capacity of each state to develop on the basis of its own best interests. the range of areas of influence and intervention in the development of this sector confirms its enormous complexity, and calls for prudent discussion and further in-depth reflection.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group, the lack of servers in the european union with sufficient capacity to store research data means that researchers have to go elsewhere and store data outside the european union without safeguarding data protection. the same applies to access: there are problems of access because we do not have this capacity within the framework of the european union. this project that is being presented today is an ambitious project, but for it to work at its full potential, it is necessary, first, that the system be kept in the public domain, ensuring use and access for the entire community, from researchers to small and medium-sized enterprises and other sectors of industry; second, to ensure data portability and interoperability; and third, to protect personal and research data under the legislation that we already have and not to give up a millimeter. the european cloud initiative is a key project for the digital society, but we cannot let private interests overlap and so we can actually end public-private partnerships. i think it would be a mistake in terms of access and what should be the background of this project, which i think is of extreme importance. and finally, finally, especially for the commissioner and his adviser, alfredo sousa jesus, imagine the society we would be living in today if marx had had the opportunity to share all his work on the cloud.
commissioner, we are living in an age of accelerated access to and processing of data. the digital society that is emerging in today's world is built on a magma of ever-increasing amounts of data. the european union needs a robust infrastructure for its storage, high-speed connectivity and transport capacity and computers with high processing capacity and from here we have to set priorities, we have to focus on the innovation of services for citizens, on the integration of small and medium-sized enterprises in value chains and, with this, we have to be able to improve responses in areas such as security, privacy protection, health and the qualification of people. the wifi for eu initiative, which contributes to universal, high-quality internet access, regardless of economic status and geographical location, can be used to test the promotion of inclusion associated with this initiative. i leave you, commissioner, with this challenge. and we also need to improve connectivity to other parts of the globe. the submarine cable between europe and brazil is also important for the success of this initiative.

mr president, commissioner, first of all, i would like to congratulate my colleague, the rapporteur of this important document. i have a few questions for the commissioner. i ask you whether you are aware that in 2016 over 13 billion euros were not paid and therefore not implemented in cohesion policy alone. the european funds for strategic investments are key to competitiveness, economic growth and employment. but who are responsible for this non-execution? is it the commission? is it the member states? is it the red tape? i have no doubt that we must reduce bureaucracy. i have no doubt we must also speed up: we are already in 2017 and the financial framework ends in 2020 (the cohesion policy funds). we also have to do something else, which is to promote synergies. i am the rapporteur for the juncker plan of the european fund for strategic investments. it is essential that there be a link between this fund, the juncker plan and the structural investment funds, in particular the cohesion policy funds. i 'll give you another one: as of 31 january 2017, 127 managing authorities out of a total of 564 managing authorities were still to be appointed. this is unacceptable! 127 managing authorities to be appointed? whose fault is this non-appointment? without the appointment of these authorities, there will be no payments and it is essential that the amounts from the eu budget and these funds reach the economy.

on behalf of the s&d group. in this resolution we strongly condemn the recent executions in kuwait and bahrain, countries where the judiciary has no credibility. in manama, the way the police have dealt with the protests over the past few days for the anniversary of the 2011 uprising shows that the human rights situation has worsened, with increasing violations including summary executions and torture. it is essential that the bahraini authorities stop the executions of mohammed ramadan and hussain ali moosa, who were denied a fair trial, and commit themselves immediately to abolishing the death penalty, to stop the crackdown and restore the freedom of action of the opposition party al-wefaq, to release political prisoners and human rights activists such as nabeel rajab, and to restore citizenship to sheikh issa ahmad qassim and other dissidents whose citizenship was illegally and arbitrarily revoked. for the security and stability of bahrain itself, it will be vital that the authorities finally engage in dialogue for reconciliation. this obviously implies the participation of representatives of the shiite majority population.

mr president, i would say that the problem is not that the commission is coming forward with scenarios, because that is interesting. nevertheless, the expectation will be of the commission saying that we can consider these scenarios but that there is a choice, which is the choice the commission is proposing. why is that so? because we are in such a situation, with these overlapping crises and these so—called existential crises, that the key question in the current situation is not whether we need to have better european solutions or to go backwards. this is not the central question. the central question is about how we can deliver better european solutions, because we have a majority in favour of better european solutions. i am sure that majority exists here in this house; i am sure, of course, it exists in the commission; and i am sure that majority exists among european citizens. why? because it is evident that, in order to deliver better labour conditions and better large—scale investment, we need better european solutions. the same goes for trade, defence, security and migration. so let us focus the discussion on how to deliver these better european solutions. i hope this will be the central question for the period to come.

in writing. freedom of movement and residence is a fundamental pillar of the union. it is the highest expression of european citizenship. it is the greatest success of and for europeans. it is the greatest achievement of citizens' europe. for a country like portugal, which has always looked to the outside, this achievement is of particular importance. as of 2015, there are more than two hundred thousand portuguese living in the united kingdom and who, as european citizens, have been able to benefit without being discriminated against by their nationality from all the rights available to the british. so reports that the british authorities are making it administratively difficult for europeans to stay in the uk cannot go unpunished. for the time being, the united kingdom remains a full member state and must therefore respect community rules. on behalf of the europeans, but particularly of the thousands of portuguese who live there, this parliament must ensure that the rights of european citizens are strictly respected.
in writing. – let me be clear: for the time being and at least for the next two years, the uk is a member of the eu, and therefore must apply eu freedom of movement laws. in case of breaches, the commission must launch infringement proceedings and take the necessary measures to guarantee the full and unreserved implementation of eu law and, in this particular case, the respect for one of the union’s core principles. whenever the uk conservative government finds the courage to act on the result of the referendum that it called for and triggers article 50 – nine months and counting! – the result of the exit negotiations will determine which rules will be applicable to eu citizens residing in the uk. again, let me be clear: their protection is our absolute priority, as is protection of the values that should guide every decision taken by the union. please note: the promotion of fear, hate and xenophobia is not acceptable in this union.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, equality between women and men is a fundamental principle of the european union, as we said earlier today in our debate on the gender pay gap. the equal participation of women and men in decision—making is a matter of justice, respect for human rights and good governance. this was underlined by the council in its conclusions of december 2015. on that occasion the council also noted that there is still a need to achieve de facto gender equality in the european union, which requires a multifaceted approach, including legislation, awareness—raising and positive action programmes. the council thus called inter alia on member states’ governments to promote a balanced representation of women and men in the political sphere, including nominations to high—level positions in the eu institutions. against this background, it is not surprising that the importance of ensuring gender balance in high judicial functions was also reaffirmed in the context of the reform of the general court, which was adopted in december 2015 and is currently being implemented. as you know, this reform foresees an increase in the number of judges at the general court in three stages. the first stage, the appointment of 12 additional judges, is currently being completed and the second stage is also in the process of being implemented with a view to reaching the number of 47 judges. two appointments are still outstanding. the final stage, in which the court will comprise two judges per member state, will be completed by 2019 and only then will it be possible to assess whether we have come closer to the objective of gender balance in the composition of the general court. at present, out of a total of 44 judges at the general court, ten are women and, among the 22 judges who were newly appointed in 2016, six are women. this is of course not yet satisfactory, even if there is a slight upward trend, which hopefully we will further consolidated. what can we do in order to reinforce an equal gender representation among judges at the court? of course under the treaty the appointment of judges and, by implication, the nomination of candidates, is the exclusive prerogative of the governments of the member states. neither the council nor parliament have any role in this respect. what the legislator can do – and has done in the context of the reform – is to create a legal framework which facilitates the nomination of one woman and one man by the member states. the phasing in of new judges has been conceived in a way which ensures that over time the regular terms of office of the two judges of each member state end at the same time. the idea is that this should make it easier for member states to propose one woman and one man. regarding the selection procedure, the decisive criterion for the appointment of judges is not gender but merit. the treaty provides that judges are to be chosen from persons whose independence is beyond doubt and who possess the ability required for appointment to high judicial office. candidates are screened and interviewed by an independent panel of eminent persons, which has set very high standards in terms of qualification and independence and whose opinions on the candidates’ suitability have so far always been followed by member states’ governments. this has led many member states to introduce internal procedures with an open call for candidates and an objective selection to identify the best candidate. this is a welcome development to ensure the highest quality of judges, but of course there is no guarantee that there will be a sufficient number of women among the candidates nor that the best candidate for a given position will be a woman. this brings me back to my initial point, the council conclusions of december 2015 on equality between women and men in the field of decision—making. in order to achieve de facto a more balanced representation of women and men in the general court, we now need to concentrate on different aspects, such as awareness—raising and regular monitoring of progress made. these are no doubt the very legitimate objectives behind ms delvaux’s question and i am confident that the views expressed in today’s debate will be carefully examined and taken into account as much as possible in the capitals of the member states.

mr president, mercury is one of the world's major pollutants with significant and lasting impacts on human health and the environment. according to who, there are no safe limits for exposure to this heavy metal. about two to three thousand tons are released into the atmosphere annually because of various human activities. once in the environment, mercury accumulates along food chains through a process of bioaccumulation. eating food contaminated with methylmercury - the most toxic form of the organic chemical - is a major source of contamination. the restrictions on the use of mercury, on the import, export, storage and production of products and processes are therefore entirely justified, and we even think that it would have been possible to go further in this area, as the work done by the rapporteur and the initial position of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety have shown. it would also have been important to make progress in the field of environmental decontamination, an aspect which is neglected in the proposals under consideration.

the european parliament will vote on the report i have drawn up on the implications of big data on fundamental rights: privacy, data protection, non-discrimination, security and law enforcement. i am grateful for the contribution of all the shadow rapporteurs, staff, assistants and experts who have contributed to the drafting of this report. we recognize that the data revolution is here, driven by advances in communication technologies and the ubiquity of electronic devices that allow the storage of data about our lives, collected en masse, constantly growing and used without control by public and private entities. as scientist tim berners-lee warned just yesterday, the most pressing risks associated with bulk data processing and algorithmic decision-making are many and include security breaches, unauthorized access, illegal surveillance, discrimination and price differentiation, as well as decision-making and public policy making based on poorly representative, inaccurate data. we therefore propose solutions: investment in digital literacy, creation of ethical standards and transparency in the use of algorithms, cooperation between the public, private and non-governmental sectors, focus on technologies that prioritise privacy and data security, account protection and compensation mechanisms, to name just a few. more urgent than ever is also the protection of reliable and rigorous sources of information, at a time when algorithms are having an increasing impact on the information available to citizens, which can distort, endanger, the fundamental rights to information, as well as freedom of the media and pluralism.we have in the european union the most advanced legal framework in the world for data protection and privacy, embodied in the general data protection regulation a legal certainty standard, which is beneficial and useful to the private sector in the european union and globally. it all depends now on how seriously we take its application and its fulfillment. this report gives parliament's input and points the way to a balance between the interests and fundamental rights of citizens and the needs of businesses. the public and legal debate will certainly continue.

mr president, i would like to begin by saying that it was the commission which linked this legislation to terrorism and that was nonsense. the commission genuinely believed that by making life difficult for hunters, sport shooters and collectors, it would solve some problem of terrorism. i'm sorry to say, but it won't. hunters, marksmen and collectors don't go around closing gun barrels to rob banks or plant bombs on the streets. hunters, collectors, and sport shooters value their weapons. terrorists, on the other hand, do not buy weapons in the highly regulated market, subject to registration, which ultimately allows their identification because of it. terrorists even use, for example, knives, axes, automobiles, and trucks, as in recent attacks. that is not why the commission is going to make the lives of drivers hell. i am glad that the european parliament has been able to work on codecision here. i myself have tabled many amendments and i thank mrs ana maria corazza bildt for her efforts. today we have a text which is completely different from what the commission originally intended, but the purpose makes us fear for what is the legislative effectiveness of who can make a difference there. emotionally, they wanted to connect the regulated market, the hunters, the snipers and the collectors to the fight against terrorism. that was the impetus for the introduction of the legislation here. please combat the black market and the illegal arms trade, and then perhaps you can do something...
