i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr cornelissen, for his report, which is a very good report, and i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr cornelissen, for his report, which is a very good report. this premise, which would at the very least dispense with any regulation, is obviously unrealistic and leads to serious misunderstandings. it is not true that fintec necessarily produces better finance, better products, much less more transparency or better regulation. that is why this is an area in which public regulation is urgently needed. this report does not give us the strong, clear and concrete steps that were needed. we are pleased that some concerns have been introduced, such as consumer protection, but it seems to me that more elements were needed in addition to the brakes on activity itself, which are totally absent from the report. privacy and security rights, monitoring and data protection should have been better enforced and unsolicited spam should have been banned. unfortunately, in our view, this report falls short of its regulatory responsibility and that is why we cannot vote in favour of it. in any case, i appreciate the effort you have made to incorporate all the requests we have made in relation to the matter i referred to earlier. we can't possibly agree. we will continue to work together, no doubt, in other areas.
madam president, we have a clear and consistent line to deal with brexit. so this is good news and it creates good conditions for our negotiators to start their work. in this way we can also devote more energy to dealing with our own future. in both things, brexit and our own future, the concern with citizens should be our central focus. when it comes to the roadmap for the future of european union, the starting point to define the roadmap is to build up a convincing social pillar addressing the concerns of our peoples, but particularly young people, when they are dealing today with the so-called ‘digital revolution’. we need to make sure they have a proper contract and full access to social protection. we need to build up welfare systems for the 21st century. then we also need to address our citizens’ concerns when we set new trade agreements in order to protect proper social and environmental standards. we need a new generation of these trade agreements. again, we need to address citizens’ concerns when managing the migration and refugee crisis because we know that the real solution for this is to address the root of the problem by supporting neighbourhood development and peace building. all of these must be translated into a community budget of the future. our task is immense, and that is why we should work now with a real focus on a scenario which is not one or two of five, but should be a simple one: let us work together for much better solutions on the relevant priorities.
madam president, it is often said that the worst blind man is the one who does not want to see. to those who are quick to see the elections in france and the netherlands as an expression of popular support for european union, we would remind them that the parties which have defended and implemented the policies of the european union have been heavily penalized by the electorate. it is true that some, dressed in new clothes, now want to perpetuate the same old policies. sooner or later, they will suffer the fate of those before them. the worrying advance of the far right must be measured not only by the electoral expression of the parties that represent it, but also by the dissemination of its principles, values and conceptions, and its assimilation by other forces of the political spectrum from the right to social democracy, in discourse and in community practices, in the acceptance of the restriction of rights, freedoms and guarantees, through to economic policies favouring big capital and generating profound social injustices. in the history of peoples, there are no dead ends. the alternative to the far right is not, and cannot be, the policies and politicians that open the door to the far right.
madam president, i think that the g7 meeting is the moment for truth and the moment of truth in the relationship between the european union, european union big countries, japan, canada and the united states. we have here the first big opportunity, especially the president of the commission and the president of the council, to face mr trump and to explain to him the why the european union, security – especially a firm and tough attitude towards russia – and free trade are so important to defend democracy, the rule of law, human rights and a fairly balanced globalisation process. we can’t miss this opportunity. it is essential that we ensure that mr trump can understand – because of the attitudes and of the policies of his allies that are here; these countries are the greatest allies of the united states – that he has to be very firm on russia without any ambiguity, very loud and clear, and at the same time that he has to defend our way of life, and that means also free trade and a regulated globalisation.
mr president, banking is of enormous importance, particularly in support of investment and consumption. it also has a special feature: when banks make a profit, it is distributed to their shareholders; when they do make a loss, it is borne by the taxpayers. in portugal, portuguese taxpayers have already paid more than €20 billion in losses to private banking. there is another extremely important aspect which is the ease with which managers of private banks are called to public supervisory functions in central banks and the speed with which they move into the management of those private banks. for all these reasons, and because of all these bad examples, the powers of the european central bank must be extended and its powers strengthened so that it can exercise strict banking supervision throughout the european union. only then will banks be put back in the service of the purposes for which they were created and for which they are largely benefited by the public authorities and the european taxpayers.
mr president, as one would expect, this debate on the powers and control of the ecb has become a way not of building solutions to strengthen the transparency and accountability of the central bank, but of combating the expansionary monetary policy of the european central bank. we know the plot well. all european responses to the crisis began with accusations of violating the treaties and the legal mandates of the institutions. monetary policy is no exception. the truth is that the situation of the european economy would be much worse today without the contribution of the ecb and a hasty decision to withdraw the stimulus to the economy would be a mistake, a serious mistake, which would jeopardise the recovery of the european economy. it is clear that those who oppose the ecb's expansionary monetary policy have their own interests ahead of europe's common interests, but it is these interests that must be reminded that this is not the ecb's mandate either. the ecb's mandate is to defend the euro area as a whole and we hope that it will continue to do so.
mr president, let's be honest with each other. the european central bank is both the most powerful and the least controlled central bank in the world. the problems arising from the powers of the european central bank are of three different orders. first, there is the conflict of interest between the different functions of the central bank, which is, of course, in charge of monetary policy, is a supervisor, in short, is a policeman and a thief, and no one controls it. the second problem has to do with the ecb's mandate. the ecb's mandate makes it an impartial bank in the face of the economic and social consequences of its monetary policy. the third problem is that - beyond the limits of the ecb's mandate, which simply do not exist - the ecb acts in all areas where it sees fit to act, whether in economic areas or in social areas, to the point of designing adjustment programmes by putting financial pressure on countries with governments with a different political orientation from the ecb's, as happened, for example, with greece. so to speak of democratic control, mr president, is to look at this parliament. the ecb officials come here to explain themselves whenever they want, say whatever they want; if they don't feel like saying anything, they fill us with emptiness and void. but in reality, the ecb has no control and, as i said, it has the most power and the least control of any central bank on a world scale.

congratulating the rapporteur on the joint work that has been possible. we all know that it is very important to define legally the procedures to ensure effective crossborder portability of online services and content, but this does not mean that we cannot harmonise and modernise technological infrastructures at european level and fight very strongly against digital exclusion, caused either by problems of qualification, by problems of economic difficulties or by geographical restrictions. this opinion, which i was rapporteur for in the itre committee, contains very relevant contributions from a legal and procedural point of view. it allows differentiation between service payments and licence fee payments. it ensures that creditors are bound by the contractual conditions as they exist at the date of entry into force of this regulation. it ensures the duty to provide information on the quality of services. it lays down the definitions of subscriber, consumer, member state of residence, temporary resident. it defines simplified verification mechanisms. it ensures technological neutrality. all this is very important, but as the commissioner has also said here, to take advantage of this opportunity it is important to develop a european digital identity based on digital inclusion and technological leadership, and for this it is very important to integrate this regulation with the telecoms package, with the rules on the end of roaming or with the implementation of the wifi4u initiative. only then will we be able to make portability a measure that benefits member of the commission. dear members of the european parliament, dear colleagues, good morning! i would like to begin my speech today by thanking, first of all, the regi committee, the committee on regional development of the european parliament, and the two rapporteurs, mr novakov and mrs tomašić, for these reports. first, i will talk about the report on the right balance between financial instruments and grants in the european union's cohesion policy. the commission naturally welcomes this report which is a key topic for the ongoing discussions on omnibus, the changes you are proposing to adopt in the context of the revision of the multiannual financial framework, and for the development of post-2020 policy. in our view, the european commission, an appropriate combination of grants and financial instruments is essential to make a difference on the ground, at local and regional level. i think we can all agree on a few common principles needed to achieve this successfully. firstly, the financing mix must be tailored to the type of investment, but also to the characteristics of each sector and each region. we believe that a significant part of cohesion policy has to be delivered through grants and could not be delivered otherwise when it comes to essential infrastructure such as schools, hospitals, childcare which do not have a direct profit. financial instruments are those that complement this support by targeting income-generating investments. at the same time, financial instruments should not be reserved for the rich and you know very well that we encourage all member states and provide technical assistance to all regions, including the poorest, to adopt and duplicate financial instruments. the commission is supporting all regions in strengthening their capacity to use the financial instruments, as i said. the second principle is that of accountability, of holding stakeholders accountable. i think this is key to enabling cohesion policy to have an impact on the ground. the managing authorities in our programmes are our best and closest partners without whom we could not implement our programmes. they are strategically placed to identify those combinations of grants and financial instruments that are appropriate for each region. in contrast, national and regional promotional banks benefit from fundamental expertise, which we need to leverage to strengthen our capacity to attract and realise the best investments. that is why we are proposing in omnibus to award contracts directly for the implementation of financial instruments and of course we in the commission would appreciate it if these regulations proposed in omnibus were adopted by you and then by the council as soon as possible. third principle: we need a well-defined framework to enable the development of safe, long-term projects, and so it is important that the changes we are discussing now are balanced and calibrated. as regards the own-initiative report by ms tomašić on the future prospects for technical assistance in cohesion policy, the commission naturally welcomes this report. several commission initiatives are recognised, such as taiex regio peer 2 peer. unfortunately, in my weekly visits to many member states, i often get the feeling that even the ministers are not aware of all these tools that the commission makes available, and this saddens me because, in practice, i have invented all these tools precisely to come to the aid of the member states. we have 250,000 workers on the ground in the field of european funds. they have accumulated experience, more or less; some have been working in this field for 42 years, since the regional development fund was invented, others have been working for 10 years, as in romania or bulgaria, others for four years. i strongly believe in this exchange of good practice which supports the exchange between fund managers and experts to increase administrative capacity between member states, regions and municipalities. we provide these services at the request of member states and i can assure you that we have very few requests in this regard; so if tomorrow a member state tells us it needs technical assistance on public procurement, within five days the commission is mobilising and sending the best experts from the regions who are performing in this field. so in this respect, i encourage you all to make all the tools that the commission makes available to member states, free of charge, known. therefore, i congratulate you on this report for providing valuable suggestions on the more effective use of technical assistance, a very useful tool, especially in the context of the post-2020 discussions. so i want to stress once again that we in the european commission encourage member states to adopt a targeted financial instrument approach given the difficulties and experiences they have faced in the past. for example, we suggested to member states that they use technical assistance to complete the fulfilment of preconditions - what we call ex-ante conditionalities for investment, but also for the implementation of anti-fraud and anti-corruption actions. unfortunately, member states generally want to use this technical assistance to cover staff and salary costs. i appreciate your speech and that of mr novakov, who very well highlighted the challenges of labour migration, brain drain, brain drain, the demographic challenges in less developed regions. we're here to help. you are right: training and preparation are sometimes more important than money and i am glad that both rapporteurs, including the representative of the committee on budgets, have stressed the responsibility of the member states, because when it comes to implementation, it is one hundred per cent the responsibility of these states. i asked the member states to ensure that all funds can support staff and authorities carrying out the most important tasks, including intermediate bodies, but also regional and local authorities. i believe, as you do, that we can improve the way we use technical assistance, i believe that we must support evolution, we are at the beginning of the debate on the future of cohesion policy. we are thinking, for example, of advancing technical assistance at the beginning of the funding period, not every year, so that the implementation system can be ready before the programmes start and the selection of projects - this is one of the ideas. we could also use technical assistance to support national and regional administrations to strengthen their project pool. this will help us to ensure that the implementation of projects can start as soon as funding is available because it is very important for us to learn from the lessons of the past and not start the financial cycle with all these delays that we are having now and we had seven years ago. in any case, we are also considering ideas such as better targeting of technical assistance to beneficiaries or exchanges of experience between regions. thank you very much. mr viotti also stressed the fundamental role of strategic funds and structural investment funds, and how they can be the committee on agriculture and rural development has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. mr president, in this period of convergence, the european union has entered a divergence trajectory since the year 2000 with the less developed regions and with these less developed regions moving away from the european average and the richer regions. the multiannual financial framework reflects this reality with a significant reduction in the amount of cohesion funds compared to previous financial frameworks. and the question of financial instruments is in fact another aspect of this deliberate choice to subordinate redistributive policies and agricultural policy. i should like to say that, according to the hearings we have held in the agri committee, it has been amply demonstrated that financial instruments cannot, under any circumstances, replace existing support, particularly at the level of the second pillar of the cap, and therefore cohesion cannot be a keyword. it implies means, but it also implies, and i cannot fail to mention this, the end of austerity policies, the end of the imposition of the single currency, which constrain the national states in carrying out their public investment plans and limit, in many cases, the actual use of the structural funds.
mr president, commissioner, financial instruments are a means to achieve the objectives of cohesion policy and not an end in themselves. they should be used as an additional tool for sectors where they prove more appropriate than subsidies. grants should remain the main instrument of cohesion policy, especially for small beneficiaries. for us socialists, it is clear that we will concentrate on the projects which are most important to the citizens of the european union. investments should focus on areas that can produce multiplier effects on employment and growth, such as investments in innovation, education and sustainable energy. people are the real driving force behind innovation and the establishment of just and progressive societies. for this reason, financial instruments should also contribute to strengthening the social pillar of cohesion policy. it is also necessary to improve the performance of financial instruments in the less developed regions, that is, in those regions with the highest unemployment rates, by trying to find measures, through positive discrimination, which help to reduce the asymmetries. finally, we want a cohesion policy which continues to distribute the public funds of the european union but which, at the same time, advocates strict accountability and accountability. it is crucial that clear rules are established and that technical assistance guidelines are provided to the administrations involved to overcome the problems, something which the commissioner has always advocated from the outset along with the european parliament. finally, a warm welcome to all those who defend cohesion policy as one of the best expressions of solidarity between the regions of the european union.
in writing. the european union, as a process of capitalist integration, is not geared towards economic and social cohesion but towards divergence. cohesion policy as such only came into being when there was a fear that the divergences created by free capitalist competition between economies with very different degrees of development would become unsustainable. however, the resources devoted to it over the years have never allowed cohesion policy to fulfil its function. jacques delors, more than 20 years ago, advocated the doubling of the eu budget in order to achieve cohesion. since then, the eu has grown, but the budget has shrunk. what was never enough was weakened even more. since the beginning of the century, economic and monetary union and the euro have given an even stronger impetus to divergence. the structural funds, which now want to turn to speculation to provide financial instruments, play a central role in this context. in addition to never being sufficient, its use has become increasingly conditional over time, subject to eu political priorities and strategies (liberalisation, privatisation, deregulation) rather than being geared to the specific needs of each country according to its sovereign choices.
author. - mr president, the fight against violations of fundamental rights, such as that which is taking place in zambia, involves two orders of measures. firstly, the powers and jurisdiction of the international criminal court must be strengthened and its field of action extended to include countries which have not signed the treaty of rome. the assurance that no violator of human rights will go unpunished is the greatest guarantee we can give to potential victims of these violations, because it is the greatest deterrent we can impose on violators. other measures are political and economic in nature and require a genuine and determined fight against poverty. wherever there is poverty, there will always be human rights violations, not least because poverty itself is a qualified violation of human rights.
mr president, five weeks ago, zambian opposition leader hakainde hichilema was brutally arrested in his home while he was sleeping, on charges of treason. this serious episode and the protests it sparked are the culmination of restrictions on freedom of expression and the increase in police and political violence that has been occurring in zambia since the election campaign for the presidential elections last august. the disproportionate and unnecessary violence used by the police forces at the time of his arrest, the conditions in which he is being held and the possibility of a life sentence or even the death penalty pose a threat to the rule of law and are in clear breach of zambia's international obligations. the european union must clearly condemn all forms of intimidation against those whom the government considers to be opponents and support the promotion of an open and constructive dialogue between the patriotic front and the united party for national development to help restore democracy in zambia.

in writing. i welcome the package of measures taken by the european commission to modernise european mobility and transport. improving transport is an essential component of smart land management and improving the quality of life for citizens. it is also an opportunity for european research centres and industry to lead a new technological era, with the energy transition (to electric mobility) and the new digital society as its pillars. better transport means easier interaction between people, more fluidity in the movement of goods, more economic competitiveness and better air quality, while also reducing noise and protecting people's well-being. i also stress the impact of these measures on the multimodal and crossborder interconnection of transport systems, promoting interoperability and the complementarity of solutions and alternatives. this package of proposals is a powerful catalyst for a new generation investment plan which, to quote the commission's communication, can 'provide an important stimulus for the creation of a clean, competitive and connected european mobility of the future'.
mr president, i would like to say the following. the first key point is that for this consensus on development, it is essential that countries do not enter into a purely nationalist logic and, unfortunately, what we see is that the united states has still today given a signal on sustainable development by being only focused on its national agenda. we, the more developed nations with greater economic capacity, must be open to development and cooperation with those countries where a large part of the population lives in extreme poverty. eradicating poverty, creating a middle class in these states is our responsibility, not only as full-bodied human beings, but also as political actors, who can, by doing so, solve many of the problems, which are not only those of these populations, they are also ours. if we are able to help them, we are helping ourselves and that is what this consensus we have achieved here will naturally enhance.
mr president, the european union's new consensus on development is a good indication of the limits, contradictions and perversities of the policies pursued in this field in recent years. we are faced with a general vision which, instead of respecting the specificities and legitimate sovereign choices of the developing countries, seeks to impose models of organization of society and the economy. it is thus with the defence of the role of the private sector, the prejudice against public ownership and management or the imposition of public-private partnerships, just to give a few examples. models which tend to perpetuate dependence, subordination and backwardness. it is an attempt to subordinate the european union's powers to their geopolitical interests, to their so-called security and migration policies, to their unconcealed xenophobic and neo-colonial policies. it is the credit lines at the expense of genuine development aid. it is the pushing into more debt, rather than the recognition of the unjust and illegitimate character of the debt, that demands its cancellation. a new consensus that points, however, to an old, very old way.
madam president, we are building resilience in foreign action. we need a european union with the tools and strategic autonomy to ensure internal and external security for its citizens. we therefore need a european foreign policy and a common security and defence policy, enabled by cooperation and sharing between member states, to respond effectively to transnational and global threats. we need strategic and coherent external action across the board, including a consensus for development, which prioritises poverty eradication, civil society empowerment, women's empowerment, democracy, the rule of law, respect for human rights and humanitarian principles. without it, there can be no good governance, and we cannot achieve the millennium development goals and sustainable development. the governments are working against resilience, pushing back against xenophobic rhetoric that links migrants and refugees to terrorists, when, attack after attack, it turns out that the so-called foreign fighters are in fact european citizens. making our cities resilient, particularly against terrorism, implies changing the anti-islamic self-defeating narrative; combating social exclusion, inequalities and job destruction. it also means promoting multicultural coexistence and global regulation.
madam president, in addition to everything else that the european union's foreign and security policy has to be unacceptable, there is a certain cynicism, a certain hypocrisy, in this overall strategy that defines resilience as one of the priorities of the european union's external action. the european union, which promotes aggression, interference and the destabilisation of sovereign countries, then elects as a strategic priority the state and societal resilience of those who are attacked. turning to the east and the south, this strategy has in ukraine and syria a full and exemplary demonstration of its objectives and its consequences. it would be better if the priority were to be given to scrupulous respect for international law, the charter of the united nations and the principles enshrined in it: from respect for national sovereignty to the peaceful settlement of conflicts. it would be better not to try to make a so-called development aid the biome for the assertion of the imperial ambition and geostrategic interests of the european powers and their economic groups.
the world has committed itself to the sustainable development goals. of these, the fourteenth concerns the oceans. sustainable development is based on three pillars that cannot be ranked. as provided for in the full text of the united nations, opportunities for development must be given to remote communities and island states. at the important meeting in new york next week, a number of states and organizations will commit to the sustainable use of the oceans. it is essential to move from political commitments to concrete action. the way the european union and its member states are getting involved, on a voluntary basis, strengthens my pride in being european. we know that the united states of america is blocking agreements and decisions committed to sustainable development. this is another reason why the european union should take the lead and show its commitment in this matter. this is not just my appeal, it is a cry of future generations.
madam president, dear commissioner, in 2016 the european union set out the joint agenda for the future of our oceans. in 2015, the leaders of g7 agreed on a declaration that highlights the importance of oceans governance science? the 2030 agenda for sustainable development proposes 17 sustainable development goals, amongst them sdg 14 – the oceans goal – which aims to conserve and sustainably use the oceans, seas and marine resources for sustainable development. however, only 2.18% of the world’s oceans have been fully protected as of today. our oceans cover more than two thirds of the earth’s surface and contain 97% of our planet’s water. over three billion people depend on marine and coastal resources for their livelihoods, the majority of world trade is conducted by sea and the importance of the blue economy cannot be ignored. nevertheless, we take our oceans for granted. eighty per cent of marine pollution and litter in oceans comes from land-based sources, and maritime co2 emissions are projected to increase by 50% to 250% in the period up to 2050. put simply, oceans, seas and coastal areas form an integrated and essential component of the earth’s ecosystem. the ocean is a primary regulator of climate and weather. next month in new york the united nations’ first global conference on the oceans will take place with the aim of developing a robust implementation framework for our oceans. i believe that today, with the united states drifting away, the european union should play an important role, on the basis of its experience, and not only through its environmental policy, in developing a sustainable approach to ocean management.
member of the commission. – madam president, i would like to thank honourable members for their valuable contributions. starting with mr wałęsa, i agree that it is time to take more concrete action. that was the idea behind vice—president mogherini and myself launching the ocean governance communication last november. this is a very important way of moving forward, focusing our ocean governance strategy on three areas: first, to strengthen the legal framework; second, to reduce human pressures on the oceans; and, third, to continue investing in more research and more knowledge. i agree with mr serrão santos that it is important, again, to move from commitment to implementation and for the european union not to take the lead but to continue to lead, especially in the absence of the usa. i also agree with ms girling that we have come a long way but still have a long way to go. this applies not just to the european union, because this is a global issue and what we are doing is not only within the european union: we are tackling this at a high global level. i also agree with mr nicolai that agenda 2030 is an ambitious agenda. i can assure you that, in the same way that we took the lead during the negotiation process, we will continue with that lead, even more importantly during the implementation phase as well. as i mentioned, eliminating gaps in the legal framework is one of our focus areas in the ocean communication. fighting illegal, unreported and unregulated fishing (iuu) is another important aspect of our second area of priority, reducing human pressures on the ocean. i also agree that we have to walk the walk when we say that all countries and all continents have to do the same. we cannot possibly move on more, better and stronger legal frameworks if some of us have still not ratified the existing legal framework, such as the united nations convention on the law of the sea (unclos) – and we are doing our best to push the countries in question to start committing to and implementing the existing legal frameworks. with regard to an instrument on biodiversity beyond national jurisdiction (bbnj), a lot of progress has been made, and in the coming weeks one of the final preparatory meetings will take place. again, we are pushing other countries for support. with regard to international agreements, we all know what is happening in the usa. i have already mentioned elsewhere that international agreements are there to be honoured. we cannot discuss, commit to and then not honour our commitments. with regard to marine litter, this is a scourge and we are also addressing this, among other things through the new plastics strategy, which will be coming out before the end of this year. we are trying not just to address what has already happened in the ocean but to address this issue at source. we call it ‘marine litter’ but it is actually all land litter that ends up in the sea. regarding encouraging blue growth, we recognise the ‘blue opportunities’ from the oceans, but we also recognise the environmental responsibilities that go along with this, and these environmental responsibilities come into play when we talk about deep-sea mining, drilling and so on. ms goddyn, with regard to illegal fishing, using the red card is, i think, the most effective tool that we have – and we have used it. it has been effective because, when it comes to trade, we have seen countries which were traumatised when we used the red card but some of which, today, are our best partners when it comes to fighting iuu. mr millán mon, it is true that our industry in europe is one of the best regulated and, i should add, not just within european union waters but in our external waters as well. one word of caution: we do, more than anything, want to support small—scale fisheries, but unfortunately – and i say this with a little sadness – small-scale does not always mean sustainable. i think we have to focus on what is sustainable and what is not rather than looking at what is big or small. with regard to quotas and where stocks are, we are doing our best. at the moment, the only pending issue, where everyone is saying that the stocks are doing well and we can utilise a higher quota, is probably tuna. we are waiting for the new quotas to come out. new scientific advice will be coming out in the next few weeks as well. ms bonafè again mentioned the fact that, unfortunately, president trump is not complying. it was a very sad statement that we all followed today. my feeling is that this does not reflect what the whole of the usa is thinking because i know that ngos there are totally supportive, as is a section of the private sector. i know that even some of states within the usa have already come up with their own plans for ocean governance and blue growth. here again, the european union is ready to take a stronger lead and i think this is the sentiment when we talk to other continents and other countries. they are not saying that if the usa is not taking on its commitment they will not take on theirs. no, it is the complete opposite of that: the sentiment is that, if a vacuum is created by the usa, we have to work harder and to do better to fill that vacuum. ms pietikäinen, you said the same thing about taking a stronger lead, especially in terms of what we are seeing. i totally agree with you about the ecosystems. very often, when we talk about ecosystems and biodiversity, we are referring to our biodiversity and ecosystems on land, yet we know that the oceans are home to more than 70% of the planet’s global ecosystem. with regard to the arctic, we are pushing through our arctic policy – which we also launched last year – to have a fishing moratorium and to agree that no fishing will take place before there is scientific advice on this. mr faria also mentioned the areas protected in the oceans. when it comes to protected areas, it is true that we have made more progress on land than we have in the ocean. on land we have managed to reach 18% to 20% of the surface area of the eu, whereas in the oceans we have barely reached 60%. so we have to do more work, not just to identify the areas but also to see how we are going to manage those areas because simply identifying the areas without having any management plans at all is not going to get us anywhere. i agree with mr caputo that the coming conference in new york is a unique opportunity for everyone to start showing their commitment. i would add that the conference in malta in october is also a very important one. as i said in my speech, the two events are complementary and mutually reinforce one another. we are looking forward to the sequence of the other ocean conferences, the fourth one being in october. mr marius, yes, regarding our fight against iuu, you mentioned china. china is a special case because we can see that it wants to be committed and is making positive signs, with a lot of willingness to cooperate. just to let you know, we have set up the eu-china blue year this year, and tomorrow we are going to have a chinese delegation – another chinese delegation within a period of three months – to continue working on our eu—china blue year and, as i said, we will continue pushing to turn their willingness into action. i would like to thank everyone involved once again and i would like, in particular, to thank ms engström.
in writing. sustainable development goal 14, as defined by the un, concerns the conservation and sustainable use of oceans, seas and marine resources for sustainable development. this is one of those areas where the need for coordinated and shared efforts at international level is evident. the oceans and seas are indeed fundamental to our life, to our well-being and to the future of humanity and the planet as we know it. the issues under discussion such as ocean warming and acidification, coral bleaching, increasing pressure on fish stocks and the increasing amount of marine litter are relevant and require discussion and action with a view to protecting the oceans. without accepting that the eu and its policies are being shaped by a sustainability paradigm, on the contrary, we follow the concern that is at the root of this debate, which we consider necessary. a broad view of the whole range of interactions between man and the seas and oceans, and of the activities associated with them, is required. fishing is just one of the activities to be considered. there are others, such as the exploitation of mineral resources, among other examples, which have been gaining increasing centrality.
mr president, we are debating today two structural themes of the digital revolution we are witnessing in the european union and in the world. for a world in rapid change and full of uncertainties, the digital revolution is an opportunity to re-shape the european project. i welcome the report on the digitisation of european industry and its rapporteur for the proposal for a global strategy, addressing the dimensions of infrastructure, investment, innovation and skills. i also welcome the report on 5g and its rapporteur for the new technological vision it shares, combining the instruments needed to make it a reality. this week, parliament and the council reached a provisional agreement on the wi-fi4eu initiative. as your rapporteur, i made it clear that in developing the european gigabit society, we have to ensure high technological standards, promote inclusion, boost the economy and the capacity to generate value and jobs. we have an opportunity to reposition the european union in the world as a technologically leading and inclusive project and we cannot afford to waste it.
mr president, technological change is an important engine of development. however, its actual results depend on the mode of production which characterizes the society in which this change takes place. it can be used to improve the living conditions of workers and peoples, or it can be used to increase exploitation of workers, inequalities and social injustices. under the conditions of capitalist globalisation, it is the second hypothesis that prevails. the digitisation of industry, part of what some call the 'fourth industrial revolution', combines innovation and the increasing automation of production processes with global technology platforms based on networks of digital communication infrastructures. workers cannot be left out of these processes. they should benefit from the changes in the salary and benefits plan. these changes must not be used as a pretext for imposing social regression. it should not be overlooked that, in the context of the maintenance of the neoliberal policies in force, these processes tend to deepen social and national inequalities.

madam president, freedom of the press and information, together with freedom of expression, are inalienable values of democracies and therefore a cornerstone of the european treaties. they enable citizens to exercise their right to be informed, to scrutinize and to judge the actions of those who wield power and influence. the european court of human rights itself has established the positive obligation of states to guarantee media pluralism. the concentration of media control in the czech republic is therefore a matter for the european institutions and not just, as the oligarch andrej babis argues, an internal matter for the czech republic. it is worrying that babis, as a media owner, leader of a political party and owner of one of the country's leading business conglomerates, tries to influence the journalistic content of the publications he controls. even if he did not intervene, his power could induce self-censorship by journalists. the concentration of media and the buying by opaque economic powers is a phenomenon which has been occurring in several member states, including my own, portugal. in 2013, this parliament adopted a resolution on the general framework for media freedom in the european union, calling for robust standards of control and transparency of media owners. we have asked the commission for measures to support the independence of the media and regulatory agencies, including a minimum legislative framework for all member states, but little or nothing has been done and this debate is therefore all the more relevant. the media can't be a business like any other. its integrity and public utility are directly related to the diversity and plurality of information, which need special protection, especially in times when strategies of hybrid warfare and electoral interference are based on the dissemination of false or biased news. we must ensure that this special protection is guaranteed by the european union and our democracies.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, freedom of the press is a pillar of the rule of law. it's a basic right. we enshrined it in the charter of fundamental rights of the european union, and we did so because a true democracy needs informed citizens who can scrutinise their representatives. now, without freedom of the press, that is not possible. unfortunately, in recent times we have been faced with frequent and unthinkable violations of this fundamental right. the so-called fake news has gained a worrying dimension and today contaminates much of the public space, with far-reaching consequences and motivations often of a geo-strategic nature. fake news is a virus that is now infecting the freedom of the press. freedom of the press is also often curtailed by the deliberate action of governments. look at the measures that have been passed by the governments of erdogan in turkey, maduro in venezuela, putin in russia, and all the countries that persecute journalists and shut down newspapers, radio, television and online public debate spaces. many journalists pay the price of freedom and some even with their lives. the concentration of the media in one person, especially a political agent with governmental ambitions, is also a cause for concern, and this is precisely what is happening in the czech republic. it seems clear to me that the use of the media for political gain puts press freedom at risk. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, let me be clear. the essential thing here today is to reaffirm that freedom of the press is written into our political dna, that it is the cornerstone of our democracies and a fundamental right of european citizens. and above all, when it is threatened, it is our duty to defend it and there is no babiš in the czech republic, erdogan, maduro, putin or fake news that should divert us from this plan.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, commissioner nordström, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to start by congratulating mrs kaufmann on her excellent work and i know that this is also the view of my colleague petri sarvamaa, who was the shadow rapporteur for the epp. in 1995, when we established a standard visa, we incorporated into the community sphere a policy initially developed by the states party to the schengen agreement. their uniformisation was and remains essential in an area of free movement. a visa is an authorization to legally cross an external border and entitles its holder to stay in our territory. for the schengen area, the external border, although legally a national border, is in practice a european border. it is therefore a european policy to guarantee their authenticity and security. it has to be one of our priorities. schengen requires a set of european policies that can ensure its proper functioning and ensure mutual trust between its member states. schengen is the management of external borders, it is the common asylum policy, it is the visa policy, it is the return policy, it is police cooperation, it is judicial cooperation and it is the schengen information system. it is also interesting that the added value of this area has led ireland, which is not a schengen state, to want to take part in the adoption of this regulation. this is also a sign of the times, where countries that sometimes make the speech that it is better to be outside, when confronted with the concrete benefits, prefer to be an integral part and to be inside. the importance of this regulation also tends to be seen in the light of the fight against crime and illegal immigration. in recent years, we have seen a rise in visa counterfeiting, which is putting our border security at risk. despite this, and despite the fact that it was consensual and necessary, the file was dragged through the legal services, the commission and the council. thanks to the negotiating team in this house, thanks to mrs kaufmann once again, and thanks to the support of the maltese presidency and also of the commissioner, this regulation was finally adopted. i welcome this result. our borders are safer.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, this report by mrs joëlle bergeron raises a question about one of the fundamental dimensions of solidarity, namely the guarantee to all citizens of the conditions for a dignified end to life. it is on this point of solidarity that i want to speak now. solidarity is today a political imperative of the modern states of law, perhaps their most impressive social mark. it does not stem from a religious impulse, from fear or love of a deity on the part of other human beings, but is rather an emanation of human dignity which imposes itself as a political requirement on society as a whole, that is, on the state itself. solidarity as an ethical and political imperative is an achievement of the enlightenment, it is one of the most emblematic syntheses of the values of the european renaissance and the enlightenment. unfortunately, we see it being politically devalued almost everywhere, either by passing on to private institutions the obligations it imposes on the states, or by simply ignoring them. it is therefore necessary, indeed imperative, to strengthen the political content of solidarity by means of normative measures to make it effective in the lives of the most vulnerable citizens of the european union, especially the elderly. political decisions are therefore needed to ensure that our fellow citizens can fully exercise one of the fundamental rights of the union: the right to move about freely. only in this way will another value of the charter of fundamental rights of europe be valued: citizenship. i am therefore, to quote a well-known phrase, asking parliament and the commission to take this small political and regulatory step, which will be a giant step in the lives of millions of our fellow citizens.
mr president, i voted in favour of this resolution in recognition of the fact that the european union should take a leading position in the implementation of the 2030 agenda for sustainable development. adopted by the un in september 2015, it constitutes an ambitious framework for achieving sustainable development and eradicating poverty. in this decision, the fact that we are living in a time of increasing uncertainty and ambiguity, which leaves a lot to be desired about the future of some of the consensus already reached on energy and climate objectives, which aims at a green, sustainable economy, giving priority to the fight against climate change. the world has changed since the last european consensus on development was adopted in 2005. today we are faced with demographic changes worldwide, associated with economic, social and environmental changes, and the levels of poverty and inequality, as well as their geographical distribution, have changed. this new consensus aims to find lasting solutions to complex global challenges and the european union and its member states should be firmly committed to implementing the 2030 agenda for sustainable development, promoting the universal values of democracy, good governance, the rule of law and human rights for all.

member of the commission. – mr president, dear rapporteur, honourable members, we have been making tangible progress in our efforts to improve fisheries governance in the mediterranean. in 2016 we launched the catania process and this highlights the urgency in addressing precarious situations for our fisheries. it also made clear the need to join efforts across the region and to set in motion a coherent plan of action for the next ten years. i think mr affronte just said that we cannot think about the solution for the mediterranean without the third countries, and i think that is really on—spot. to do this, we organised a ministerial conference in march in valletta and during this the ministers from 13 countries around the mediterranean signed the malta medfish4ever declaration. this is a declaration that stresses environmental sustainability, social cohesion and viable economic perspectives. we now have an action plan. this provides a clear set of objectives and targets, against which we can measure our progress. it addresses critical fisheries—related issues including data collection and scientific evaluation, the eco—system—based approaches in the fight against illegal, unreported and unregulated fishing and support for small—scale fisheries. the plan also deals with pollution and transport and their impact on fishing resources. now, the plan is in place but we must translate our words into concrete actions at three levels: at eu level, at multilateral level and across eu and non—eu countries. at eu level we will continue to implement our common fisheries policy. this will require progress on multiannual plans. the plans, like the small pelagic fisheries in the adriatic will mark the first test on how genuine our commitments are. we are also working on a plan for demersal fisheries in the western mediterranean and we are cooperating with the member states on new national measures, in particular in the gulf of lion and the jabuka/pomo pit. at a multilateral level we will strive to make further progress in the general fisheries commission for the mediterranean at the plenary next october. this will include the adoption of a regional plan of action to fight illegal, unreported and unregulated fishing. it will also involve the establishment of a pilot project of an international inspection scheme in the straits of sicily. last, we must continue to ensure solidarity with non—eu mediterranean countries. we will do this by supporting them in their structural reforms in fisheries, through the eu neighbourhood funding instruments. our joint political partnership has the power to create a healthy environment, a thriving blue economy and a sustainable fishing sector in the mediterranean. i am very pleased to see that the report by mr marco affronte is going in that same direction. we would like to congratulate the european parliament for this initiative. we see that your report is addressing issues that are also important to the commission: the role of small-scale fishing, measures for third countries, socio-economic aspects, the need for better data and science-based allocation of fishing opportunities and impact of other human activities. the majority of the issues highlighted in your report are directly addressed in the medfish4ever declaration. both are in the interest of fishermen and citizens.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the social partners are able to take part in the negotiations. mr president, the socioeconomic situation of the mediterranean fisheries, which are mainly small-scale, has been deteriorating due to reduced yields, depreciation of fish stocks, restrictions on fishing for certain species, and higher fuel costs, which have contributed, among other things, to an increase in fishing effort, with the inevitable increase in risk for fishermen. measures are needed to safeguard small-scale fisheries by involving fishermen and their associations and unions in decision-making processes, promoting measures, including the channelling of european funds, for the renewal and modernisation of fleets, improving working conditions, contributing to economic and environmental sustainability, including through the reduction of fishing effort. measures that give value to work and labour regulation, incomes, social protection, recruitment of young people and vocational training, as well as the role of women in fisheries, who represent 12% of the workforce in the sector. measures are required to substantially and objectively improve safety with a view to reducing high accident rates.
mr president, i would also like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr marco affronte. inherent in the definition of sustainable development is the need not to hierarchise the three pillars that underpin it. so environment, economy and society have to be equated in sync. in the mediterranean fisheries, the environmental pillar has been ignored for too long technical measures such as operating times or mesh sizes have been tested for years without success. now is the time to act and protect the environmental pillar that i think is the weakest of this triad. it is absolutely necessary to respect a set of restricted reserve areas and spring recovery areas and to impose a quota system for the most important fish species in the mediterranean. at present, sea bass is the only good-conditioned marine resource in the mediterranean, and the only one with quotas in place. the failure to implement a quota system as a matter of urgency will be a huge liability and will guarantee the bankruptcy of the fishermen.
mr president, commissioner, fishermen are facing a very serious economic crisis and the european commission must create measures and mechanisms to protect fishermen's income. and i think that this is done in four dimensions: first, training, both in the arts and in the science of fish farming. secondly, it also implies a commitment to strengthening the position of fishermen in the value chain. it is unacceptable that fishermen bring home pennies and that large-scale distribution has large profit margins and that consumers pay fortunes for the fish they consume. and this implies, and this is my third point, the need for us to strengthen even more the producer organizations. and finally, fourthly, we must invest in the strengthening and creation of a compensation fund for wages when fishing is prohibited. i am not referring to the existing biological stops, but to the adverse climatic conditions. that is fundamental. i am also referring to environmental disasters and to contamination by biotoxins. if these measures are implemented, we will have more social justice in fisheries too.
mr president, commissioner, the le grip report has the great merit of bringing to the fore the problem of the pension gap between men and women in the european union. despite the increase in women's participation in the labour market in recent decades and the reduction of gender pay gaps, gender gaps in the labour market and in domestic activities are still large. the fact that, on average, women's pensions in the european union are 38% lower than men's pensions, in addition to obviously increasing the risk of poverty for older women, represents an accumulation of gender imbalances and inequalities throughout working life. in addition to lower wages, it is women who see their career path and thus the continuity of their contributions more often interrupted, either because they have more family responsibilities or because they are the ones who perform more informal and precarious work. gender equality and non-discrimination are fundamental principles of the european union. reducing the gender gap in pensions requires changing the patriarchal model of social protection systems designed 70 years ago and preventing and combating the double exclusion that affects women in working life and later in the social protection to which they are entitled in old age.
mr president, the disparity in the value of pensions between men and women has objective causes. the systematic discrimination against women throughout their working lives, in maternity, in access to work and in wage values, and the increased vulnerability to precarious work and poverty, are added to the consequences of the austerity and right-wing policies which impose a brutal attack on social and labour rights throughout europe and in portugal. the fight against low-wage policies, the principle of equal pay for equal work, the promotion of collective bargaining, the fight against precariousness, the protection of maternity, the creation of public networks of care for children and the elderly, the full support of a public, universal and solidarity-based social security system as the only guarantee of the right to a decent retirement pension are just some of the urgent measures needed to reverse these disparities in the future. it is urgent to implement the pension update and to reject any link between the increase in average life expectancy and retirement age.
mr president, last week in new york the united nations conference on the oceans took place, and i had the honour of participating in it as head of the european parliament delegation. it was a real turning point in the recognition that the ocean, our ocean, is crucial to the future and to humanity in all its diversity. this conference aimed to support the creation of conditions for the implementation of the 14th un sustainable development goal, dedicated to the sustainability of the oceans. the european parliament delegation participated in several bilateral meetings with representatives of the world bank, the united nations environment programme, the convention on biological diversity and the delegations of the usa, china and brazil, in the framework of improving coordination and cooperation between international organisations and creating partnerships in the field of oceans. 193 un member states have approved by acclamation the declaration 'our ocean, our future: call for action', which commits all countries to work towards the sustainability of the oceans. ladies and gentlemen, the european union has made important commitments in the political recognition of the importance of conserving marine resources and ecosystems. the urgent task now is to act in defence of the oceans and to implement a new and ambitious international ocean governance.
the european union cannot hesitate to take the lead in the process of harmonisation and combating climate change. the report under discussion today reinforces the european union's commitment to the goals of the paris agreement and is given added importance by the decision taken by the us administration to withdraw the united states from the agreement. this regulation sets binding greenhouse gas emission targets for member states covering sectors of the economy not covered by the ets. these sectors account for almost 60% of total emissions in the eu in 2014 and the setting of national targets was done with a fair process. this is a shift that will provide opportunities for jobs, growth and investment for europe, while mitigating climate change and requiring changes in behaviour, business incentives and investment. that is why it is essential that the options contained in this report can have broad support in this parliament. we have made a clear choice to lead the transition to a sustainable economy and society. to hesitate in the current global context could mean to lose everything.
mr president, commissioner, the paris agreement provides that the parties take measures to conserve and, if necessary, to strengthen sinks and reservoirs of greenhouse gases, including forests. this proposal is an integral part of the implementation of the european union's commitment under the paris agreement to reduce emissions across the economy. ladies and gentlemen, we are in a period of great uncertainty, of incomprehensible denial of the obvious effects of climate change by those who, like the united states, are the biggest contributors to emissions into the atmosphere. the union's top priority is to create a resilient energy union that prioritises energy efficiency and provides secure, sustainable energy at prices that are affordable for citizens, as well as to implement policies to reduce emissions and replace fossil fuels. and that, ladies and gentlemen, is our priority if we are to lead the way in meeting the paris commitments. i believe that only through ambitious climate action can we contribute to rescuing a planet that is sustainable for life in time. i would like to congratulate mr gerbrandy on this excellent commitment document and say that europe cannot now back down on the commitments it made in paris. europe now has a unique opportunity to show the world its leadership in this field.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group, at a time of stalemate in which the deep crisis of this model of european union integration is all too evident, this reflection paper represents a real breakthrough. at a time when a thorough reflection on the role of economic and monetary union is needed in this long process of social and economic divergence, this document actually proposes to examine in depth all the factors which have been at the root of this divergence. in my city of aveiro, workers who are fighting for their rights and are on strike today are feeling the effects of this neoliberal european union dominated by the multinationals. europe does not need a finance minister. it will not be a single representation in the imf that will solve the problems of unemployment and economic dependence. budgetary capacity, with all the associated constraints, will only reinforce the oppressive character of the euro area and limit the ability of member states to respond to its problems in accordance with their models and in accordance with the will of their people. it is now clear that the eurozone is a political project designed to impose a neoliberal agenda, and therefore what is needed is not its strengthening but rather its dissolution in a process that includes the recovery of the monetary sovereignty of the member states and the renegotiation of public debts, the increase of which cannot but be associated with the euro and its economic governance.
madam president, the european union can only have a future if the eurozone becomes an area delivering prosperity and upward convergence for all its member states and for all the other states which are planning to join. we need to stop this machine for divergences which are not only economic; they are social and political. so we welcome the commission’s proposal with a plan which, finally, recognises that the problems of the eurozone are not only financial stability, but also growth, job creation and upward economic and social convergence. of course, member states do need to engage in an effort of their own in reforms and investments in the framework of the european semester. but exactly because they are confined by a common fiscal discipline, they must also count on an instrument to support this upward convergence. we hope that this instrument will be created in the upcoming next budgetary framework. we need to have it as soon as possible, otherwise we cannot reverse this logic of divergence. beyond that, of course, we need to equip the eurozone with the proper fiscal capacity and this fiscal capacity should be based on democratic governance. the proposal of having a minister of finance, who is also a vice-president of the european commission makes sense from our viewpoint, but this will suppose full accountability before the european parliament. the proposal coming now from the commission to have an agreement involving parliament, the commission and the council to discuss this makes sense and we are available to do so.
mr president, commissioner, you are a doctor and therefore you will understand what i am about to say. glyphosate is the most widely used herbicide in the world, with 76% of its use worldwide in agriculture, forestry, urban and garden applications. glyphosate and its residues have been detected in water, soil, food and the human body. the who has classified this herbicide as a probable human carcinogen and a proven carcinogen in laboratory animals. glyphosate also has a negative effect on the oceans, where everything depends on the phytoplankton at the top of the food chain. the stop glyphosate initiative aims to put pressure on the commission with two objectives: to ensure the transparency and independence of processes in the reuse of pesticides and to impose mandatory deadlines for the progressive reduction of the use of all pesticides. the authorisation process for glyphosate in the union has been a muddy one, with conflicting studies, secret documents and conflicts of interest revealed, which is why we need a study based on all available scientific data regarding the carcinogenicity of glyphosate as well as possible endocrine disrupting properties. under the precautionary principle, they will continue to press the commission not to renew the authorisation of glyphosate in the light of the overriding public interest of protecting the environment and public health.

commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the guidelines of the european union on human rights which we are discussing here today do not, as we know, have, nor could they have, a legally binding character. however, the european union, having regard primarily to its own founding values, has a moral obligation to strive for their implementation and to create the conditions so that, with its political influence and negotiating capacity, they can be respected and implemented in a variety of situations. we know that's not always the case. we see attacks on freedom of expression with unprecedented frequency all over the world, from the least serious to those that can only be considered as absolutely unacceptable, such as the murders of journalists that have occurred in mexico or the detention and prosecution of dozens of members of the media that have occurred in a neighboring country like turkey. it is essential that the european union continues to use all the instruments at its disposal to combat these and other attacks on freedom of expression, and i believe that in this area we have a fundamental instrument in the trade agreements we have with various countries in the world.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to welcome the honourable members here, including the commission, and say that the efsi, the european fund for strategic investments, is meeting its objectives in the investment and innovation window. it is in accordance with the plan drawn in the window of the small and medium-sized enterprises. it's going beyond even the best of predictions. the evidence is that over eur 190 000 000 has already been mobilised in the european union and over 420 000 small and mediumsized enterprises have been supported. it is in this way that we can create more jobs by promoting investment and at the same time increase the competitiveness of our economy, without ever forgetting social, economic and territorial cohesion. however, the european fund for strategic investments does not have the visibility it deserves in the light of the results it has produced. we need to improve communication and the visibility of this fund. the proof of success is that we are already negotiating the extension of the european fund for strategic investments to eur 500 000 000 and its extension until 2020. this does not mean that this fund, its implementation and, in particular, the implementation by a key player, the european investment bank, cannot and should not be improved. of course, knowing the projects that are not pre-selected, and here there is an attitude that can be discriminatory in law, but an attitude that is in the power of the european investment bank. it is important to know at least the projects that have not been entered into the system, the projects that have not been pre-selected. it is also important and essential to have a better geographical distribution, a better geographical balance, as far as projects are concerned. it platforms is essential for this purpose, so that they can include small projects. the need for platforms for crossborder projects is also something that needs to be addressed. the digital union is important, the energy union is essential and in this way too, in addition to promoting the internal market, in addition to promoting interconnectivity and efficiency, we would be contributing to objectives for the whole of the european union. this fund is an additional fund, it is also a complementary fund, but it is important and the platforms are a means where this can be achieved, so that the funds, in particular those of cohesion policy, can be better used in conjunction with the structural funds. it is important that the additional aspect be reinforced and respected. it is also essential that the economic situation of less developed regions and regions in difficulty, for example, be taken into account in the selection of projects. finally, the advisory hub is essential to
