president-in-office of the council. – madam president, this year a number of countries in southern europe have experienced particularly severe and devastating forest fires. many people have lost their lives. portugal and spain have been among the most affected countries. on behalf of the council, i would like to express my deepest condolences and sympathy to the relatives of the victims, to thank the countries which have provided assistance and also to thank the commission for its coordination efforts. last but not least, let me warmly thank all the firefighters for their huge commitment and effort. as honourable members are aware, the union civil protection mechanism, which we jointly adopted in 2013, provides for a number of measures to prevent, prepare for and respond to disasters. this includes the european emergency response capacity, which is a voluntary pool of member states’ assets pre—committed for immediate deployment inside or outside the eu. several member states have registered their forest fire fighting capacities in this pool. these resources have been deployed a number of times to provide assistance in combating forest fires across southern europe. however, despite significant efforts at national and eu level to prevent forest fires, we have to stay prepared and ready to respond in a more efficient manner to these catastrophes. at the same time, a swift and efficient response is dependent on costly, and therefore limited, resources. eu solidarity might prove its limits when similar disasters occur simultaneously in a number of countries. of course, when the commission proposes the mobilisation of the eu solidarity fund, the council will examine such a proposal as a matter of utmost urgency. finally, the council has attentively examined the commission’s recent interim evaluation report on the union civil protection mechanism. it includes a number of recommendations for further application of the mechanism. some of them are particularly relevant for the prevention of, and response to, forest fires. this evaluation has marked the starting point of reflections on the future framework of the union civil protection mechanism which the council will continue in the coming months. in this context, the presidency has taken note with interest of the recent announcement, reiterated yesterday by president juncker, of an upcoming commission proposal to revise the union civil protection mechanism. we look forward to bringing debates on this matter forward.
member of the commission. – madam president, let me also start by expressing once again my condolences to the families and the loved ones of all those who perished in the terrible fires of last week. also, to those who have lost their homes in portugal and spain. it is unfortunately not the first time this year that this region has been affected by deadly forest fires. i also wish to pay tribute to the heroic firefighters – brave men and women – and ordinary citizens that stepped in to help. in 2017, the forest fire season has been one of the worst in the last decade; southern europe has been very exposed. helping member states affected to fight forest fires has been a priority for the european union. the truth is that national capacities alone are not enough. climate change makes weather conditions more extreme in natural disasters more often. this is the harsh reality. the european union civil protection mechanism was activated 16 times because of forest fires in europe since mid-june. we have never seen so many activations in so little time. capacities such as firefighting aircraft, firefighters and vehicles were deployed on nine occasions. as commissioner for crisis management, i warmly thank all the eu member states that offered assistance through the mechanism. this is a true example of european solidarity. we did our best to provide support with the means at our disposal, but let me be clear: the mechanism has specific legal and practical limitations. our role, our mandate, is to coordinate – as my dear colleagues already said – and help with the transport costs only. we don’t have our own means, therefore we have to admit that sometimes our contribution is not sufficient. i would like to be very sincere: sometimes it was too little and too late. this is why we have already been exploring in the previous months ways to strengthen the mechanism. it was one of my top priorities. president juncker mandated me – and i want to thank him for this – to come forward with concrete proposals next month on how, together with our member states, we can do more to better respond, prevent disasters and be better prepared for them. this is not only – i would like to clarify this – about forest fires: just days ago, we had hurricanes hit ireland and the uk. floods have struck eastern and northern european member states in the recent past, and earthquakes have consistently shaken italy and greece and other countries. so we owe this initiative to our citizens, to the european citizens. it is now a priority of the commission work programme, which we adopted yesterday. the proposals will be on the table before the end of november. there are three areas that i want to explore. first, through additional financial support we must increase capacities to act rapidly and effectively. second, prevention and preparedness need to be at the core of the initiative: enhanced prevention and preparedness should be a precondition for additional support. finally, we must improve the efficiency of the mechanism to enable a faster deployment of assistance during emergencies. in order to do this, i will need your support. time is of the essence. the beginning of the next flood season has already started. the forest fire season has been dramatically extended this year, and the next one is in a few months from now. it is therefore crucial that we act fast, in a spirit of solidarity and efficiency. i know i can count on the european parliament to be a strong ally to support our proposals, because we must rapidly offer solutions to our citizens. they expect more from us as european institutions. in addition to the eu actions in the field of civil protection, let me also mention another important tool: the european solidarity fund. as you know, the european solidarity fund can provide valuable financial assistance to help cover a part of the cost created by disasters. support is subject to fulfilling two conditions. first, total direct damage should exist as a threshold which is specific for each member state or region; second, national authorities have to submit an application within 12 weeks of the start of the disaster. portugal has already submitted an application covering the forest fires that occurred between june and august. the commission will soon finalise its assessment; we are working on the basis of significant support from the solidarity fund. in parallel, we are currently discussing with portugal the possibility to extend their application to also include the most recent fires of october. for the recent fires in spain, notably in galicia, the damage has not been fully assessed yet. the commission stands ready to provide guidance and support to the spanish authorities, should they intend submitting an application. in terms of operational programmes, the commission also rapidly adopted the proposal from portugal to modify the original 2014-2020 central operational programme through internal relocation of the european regional development fund, erdf. we added two new actions. first, the replacement of the productive capacity of the smes that were lost during the fires, with a total erdf budget of eur 25 million. second, the replacement of public municipal infrastructure equipment with a maximum budget of eur 20 million. in addition, in portugal, the operational programme for sustainability and resource efficiency provides the possibility to finance a range of investments to strengthen disaster resilience, prevention and preparedness. in spain, the operational programme galicia provides the possibility to finance investments in early detections, prevention and civil protection.

in writing. the fires that ravaged portugal on 14 and 15 october have caused the loss of four dozen lives and millions of euros in material damage. moreover, they have dealt a severe blow to the portuguese forest. in particular, the vast area burned, equivalent to the total recorded in portugal in many of the previous years, deserves to be highlighted. these unfortunate events call for a deep discussion about the reasons, the immediate response, but also what we can do to prevent tragedies like this from happening again in the future. the possibility of setting up a european civil protection force has been raised more and more frequently. such a reflection should be welcomed now, as it would be an efficient way of ensuring a rapid and adequate response to the emergency support needs of the member states. in addition, this force would, in effect, embody one of the union's founding principles: solidarity. of course, other measures must be taken. because, as we all know, it's not enough to mourn tragedy, it has to be avoided. and in the case of portugal, this involves a forest reorganisation effort, which will only succeed if it is based on a broad national political consensus.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, road safety remains one of the european union's priorities in combating the scourge of death and accidents on europe's roads, which affect hundreds of thousands of families every year. to achieve the goal of zero fatalities, we must move forward with concrete and effective measures, in conjunction with the member states, on vehicle safety, road infrastructure and driver behaviour. in these three areas, there is still a lot to be done: the fight against alcohol on the road, the remodelling of infrastructure and the development of safe mobility plans for pedestrians and cyclists, especially in urban areas, and the mandatory introduction of driver assistance systems such as e-call, which is already a reality and was mentioned by my colleague olga sehnalová. road safety cannot be for those with more money. it should not be only the higher end cars that have manufacturer-provided assistance mechanisms such as emergency braking, tyre pressure control, seat belt system, etc. these technologies, which help to reduce road fatalities, should be made compulsory for all vehicles. ten years ago, the industry argued that free e-call for all passenger cars would be a disaster for vehicle prices. well, that didn't happen. a simple automatic seat belt adjustment system mandated to prevent neck injuries could save thousands of lives and comes at a paltry cost. i hope that the european commission will take account of the excellent recommendations of parliament in this report and that we can legislate intelligently to change the paradigm of deaths per year on european roads: from 25 000 to zero.

member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, the commission attaches very high importance to the conservation of posidonia oceanica meadows, also known as neptune grass or posidonia beds. this is an essential habitat as a nursery and refuge for commercial and non—commercial species alike, and it provides a major source of food, regulates nutrient cycles and prevents coastal erosion. impressively, posidonia oceanica meadows alone bury some two 2 000 tonnes of carbon each year; it would be interesting to know how this phenomenal seaweed compares with our carbon emissions saving targets or the shift to electric vehicles. posidonia beds are a priority habitat type under the habitats directive, and member states have designated around 30% as natura 2000 sites to protect them. other measures, such as national protection measures and environmental targets, are set under the marine strategy and water framework directives. in addition, the common fisheries policy prohibits the use of certain fishing gear – like trawl nets – above posidonia seagrass beds and offers member states special fishing management tools to be used in protected areas or essential habitats. despite these efforts, in 2013, the status of posidonia beds was considered unfavourable in four of the seven member states where this habitat type occurs. and while information on trends is scarce, research studies point to regression rates in the order of 10% over the past 50 years, with losses mostly observed in the western and northern mediterranean. the commission expects further information to be provided by member states in 2018, when they will report on their efforts to achieve good environmental status in marine waters. the decline in the population of neptune grass is usually linked to coastal development, mechanical damage from illegal trawling, anchoring, beach nourishment, dredging, eutrophication and other forms of pollution. to restore posidonia beds, member states must establish site-level conservation objectives and restoration measures. new protected areas are also often designated. compliance is currently being monitored by the commission. the commission is supporting the conservation of posidonia beds through the dissemination of good management practices, the co-funding of life projects, and financial support under the european regional development fund, interreg mediterranean and the european maritime and fisheries fund (emff). the good news is that investments through life have played a crucial role in ensuring the effective management and restoration of coastal ecosystems. the programme has funded more than 20 projects since 1992, focusing specifically on tackling mediterranean seagrass habitat degradation through protection and even restoration. other cross-cutting coastal life projects have also contributed to the improved knowledge and management of posidonia beds. the mid-term evaluation of the life programme, adopted on 6 november, details the careful monitoring of investments via outcome indicators and targets. the emff also provides financial support for the protection and restoration of marine biodiversity and ecosystems, which includes a common monitoring and evaluation system. the commission, in cooperation with the member states, will undertake a review of the performance of the operational programmes, the performance review, in each member state in 2019. given its phenomenal ability to absorb inconceivable volumes of carbon from the air, the returns on investing in further conservation delivers an extraordinary public good and a real contribution to shifting to the low-carbon economy. this is a prime example of both why and how we must continue to secure sufficient funding in the future.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, commissioner, cop 23 devoted 11 november to the oceans and i therefore believe that the debate on the protection of coastal marine habitats is not only timely but also extremely necessary. when we talk about protecting these critical habitats for the conversion of carbon dioxide into oxygen, we have to talk about seagrasslands, which are vital ecosystems in terms of biodiversity and which provide many relevant services to communities and the economy. the oral question is in defence of the marine grass posidonia oceanica, endemic to the mediterranean and one of the most productive and most important species for the maintenance of biodiversity. however, this important species of slow growth and regeneration has been declining dramatically in recent decades due to human activity, eutrophication and other forms of pollution, as well as as a result of climate change and damage caused by anchoring vessels. at the our ocean conference it was estimated that the total annual value of the mediterranean's natural assets will be €4.66 trillion, representing about 20% of global annual gdp. however, a strong and sustainable blue economy depends on the preservation of marine and coastal ecosystems and our ability to restore them. i therefore consider it essential to ask you, commissioner, the following questions: what is the current situation and future evolution of marine coastal habitats, in particular with regard to the marine strategy framework directive monitoring indicators associated with biodiversity and seabed integration? how does the commission assess the investments already made through the various research and conservation programmes in the framework of the protection of ocean posidonia, and what programmes and funding does the commission intend to put in place to protect and restore ocean posidonia in order to increase its resilience and minimise the impact of climate change on this species? commissioner, we urgently need robust measures if we want a sustainable future for the mediterranean and i therefore believe that the answer to these questions is essential if we are to devise a coherent strategy for the protection of the mediterranean ecosystem.
on behalf of the s&d group. commissioner, as has been said here, coastal marine habitats are fundamental to the conversion of carbon dioxide into oxygen, to the productivity of marine resources, to the protection of coastal areas and to the other essential ecological services they provide to the planet. coastal marine habitats are those most threatened by human activity, either because of their proximity or because they are the first places to be affected by activities on land. the european union has, it is true, invested significantly in funding scientific studies and programmes for the conservation and restoration of the marine environment. however, in a recent nature article from 2015, it is estimated that in the last 50 years, there has been a 34 percent reduction in the oceanic posidonia meadows in the mediterranean. the case of ocean posidonia is just one example of how disruption of the marine environment can have significant impacts on an entire ecosystem, so it is of crucial importance to monitor and evolve key species, such as this seaweed. monitoring the evolution of the conservation status of key species for conservation, the impact of investments in the conservation and restoration of marine habitats and improving their protection are key to effective and continuous protection of the marine environment. knowing and communicating the results and improvements of conservation and recovery investments to all european citizens and, in particular, raising awareness of the importance of these investments is essential if the work done is to be even more positive in protecting marine habitats and species. climate change, which has also been mentioned here, is a reality, we all feel the impact of this change on a daily basis, so the protection and recovery of species such as the ocean posidonia, which play important roles in coastal protection and other ecological services, is essential. the protection and recovery of habitats and species are actions that cannot be dissociated from the commitments to reduce carbon emissions in order to overcome climate change. the european union, it must be admitted, has been at the forefront and committed to combating climate change. but it must be borne in mind that the knowledge, protection and restoration of marine habitats, in particular coastal habitats, is an important point in combating these changes. i conclude by stressing the need to improve knowledge and monitoring of ocean posidonia, but also of other key species crucial for the conservation and preservation of marine habitats, which provide so many fundamental ecological services to the planet.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group. mr president, coastal marine habitats and the species of fauna and flora that occur there are of high ecological and conservation value. the mediterranean populations of oceanic posidonia, as in general the banks with vascular provision in shallow waters, as is also the case of the zostera, for example, both in the mediterranean region and in the atlantic region, are important refuges of biodiversity, are important in carbon sequestration, are important in regulating nutrient cycles, in the elimination and recycling of polluting waste, among other functions. the threats to these habitats are essentially associated with anthropogenic action. commissioner, it is important in this debate to discuss what actions and resources that can be mobilised from the european union can contribute to the cause of conserving these habitats. it should be noted that, although these are habitats with a threatened status and protected by community legislation, there is to date no specific financial instrument of the european union for the management of natura 2000 sites, which jeopardises conservation objectives and the recovery of degraded habitats. it was important, commissioner, for example, that we start by closing this gap.

mr minister, mr commissioner, tax evasion is a crime, whatever the means used to achieve that result. just as criminal are the conduct of those who, in order to obtain illegal advantages, use the ways that the law has forbidden, as are those of others who, in order to achieve the same result, use ways other than those that the law has foreseen and forbidden. tax evasion is also morally repugnant, not only because it deprives states of the means necessary to carry out their social functions, but above all because it is carried out by very profitable companies and very rich people, some of whom are even presented as models of public virtues, as the paradise papers now reveal. let us be clear, mr minister, mr commissioner: the eu must quickly establish a system of sanctions to be applied to countries whose financial systems are complicit in tax evasion, so that these debates do not turn into mere routine and meaningless theatrical performances. helping someone eat a crime shouldn't go unpunished.

the european union must reject all tax havens that exist for laundering money, concealing illicit profits, helping to raise the proceeds of crime. you must repudiate them inside and out, without doubt or state of mind. a blacklist, a real blacklist, is essential. it means a blacklist that is exhaustive and not a blacklist that is minimalist. to condemn in speeches what is permitted in practice is simply hypocrisy. however, it should be stressed that tax havens are not the same thing as more favourable tax regimes, which exist to help the most disadvantaged regions, especially the outermost regions, which do not have the same instruments for generating income, wealth and employment that many countries, fortunately for those countries, have. tax havens are not allowed, they are not acceptable in the european union. the problem is not the european union, certainly not the law. you'll be on the probation eventually and casually. if the law is broken in the european union in any case or at any time, then it is monitored and acted upon, not changed, as is often the case in the european union, in the shamelessness that exists in many other parts of the world. finally, a word about this committee of inquiry which was useful but was insufficient. it has shown that it is lacking, that it has far fewer instruments than many of the national committees of inquiry. there were many people, many entities who refused to hand over documents, who denied testimony when it could help uncover the truth, many who were rulers, underline and sign. madam president, after the panama papers, we have the paradise papers. once again, this data from thousands of companies and individuals shows us the urgency of concerted and coordinated action at the global level. the european union must be a pioneer, and therefore the member states, which have the primary responsibility for this, have a duty to act urgently, and also in a coordinated and concerted manner. in this area, shared sovereignty ultimately strengthens fiscal justice, fairness and transparency. an important step is the publication at european level of an offshore blacklist, which is, moreover, scheduled for before the end of the year. without this common list we will always have triangulation. tax harmonization is also essential, as is increased cooperation between tax authorities, for example, the automatic exchange of information. but tax harmonization does not mean harmonization of rates. it is also essential not to confuse more favourable tax regimes with offshore ones. article 349 of the lisbon treaty allows the outermost regions to benefit from a tax regime to attract investment, create jobs and promote development, since these regions are handicapped by their remoteness and insularity. this scheme, although it needs to be improved, has already proven to have positive effects on regional development.

which include unanimity, and this, mr bullmann, explains my diplomatic language. you only achieve results in the eu with diplomacy. i listed the initiatives and the new regimes that have been agreed on and that have been implemented during the last couple of years and it beats all the records if you compare it in the perspective of the last couple of decades. and this work is ongoing. at the ecofin ministers meeting in december, the estonian presidency will strive for the endorsement of a blacklist of non-cooperative third countries, as proposed by the conduct group. we strive for the adoption of a vat e—commerce package and we strive for the adoption of council conclusions on the taxation of the digital economy. i will conclude, perhaps, with one observation of a more general kind. some speakers here may have the impression that it is only the european parliament that takes the moral high ground here, that it is only the european parliament that stands for fair taxation, that stands for our citizens, and that governments indulge in hypocrisy, as if the member states and their governments were not interested in having fair tax systems, and as if the member states and their governments were not interested in tax revenues for their state coffers. all of us here are in the same boat and we are working towards reaching the same goals.
in writing. as the paradise papers issue has shown once again, tax evasion is one of the main obstacles to the smooth and regular functioning of our economies. it is urgent that europe takes a decisive stand against this problem. it is shameful that so many of our fellow citizens are experiencing economic hardship and facing the rigors of an economy in crisis when, at the same time, so many corporations and private citizens hide their money in tax havens to avoid paying taxes that would serve to balance the accounts of several member states. the socialists have always been at the forefront of the fight against this and other forms of tax evasion. parliament and the commission have already taken positions which help to combat this scourge. what is the council waiting for?
in writing. another scandal involving tax havens. the succession of scandals risks trivializing them before any substantive action is taken to solve the problem. this new scandal confirms the systemic nature of tax evasion. it also exposes the eu's inaction and complicity in the situation. the same people who impose poverty on workers and peoples are laying out the red carpet for big capital, which allows it to protect from just taxation the income accumulated at the expense of the exploitation of labour and speculation (a prolongation thereof). here is the demonstration of the false dilemma: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. no, i'm not going. what is needed is to put an end to these centres of economic crime which are tax havens and to increase the revenues of the states through a fair taxation of capital. greater fiscal justice and effective action to combat tax fraud and evasion are incompatible with the reigning neoliberalism imposed by the european union and governments which prove to be nothing more than representatives of the interests of large multinational companies and large financial institutions.
in writing. the european union has been involved in tax evasion scandals on a cyclical basis, with more or less legal contours, but with a strong detrimental effect on fiscal justice and the proper taxation of wealth generated on its territory. these practices have a strong impact on the credibility of the institutions and on the ability of the member states to develop their sovereignty policies and to implement the democratically legitimate social policy and economic dynamism options. the european parliament, by setting up committees of inquiry which have been mapping the contours of harmful behaviour, has made a valuable contribution to making the european commission and the member states more effective in combating tax evasion and avoidance, which penalise taxpayers and weaken the union as a global economic power. the european commission has therefore taken positive steps, but further action is needed in the fight against corruption and in active cooperation between administrations to prevent tax evasion and promote tax harmonisation within the union. these tax cooperation practices should be extended to countries which constitute the perimeter of economic interactions which may encourage fraudulent or ethically reprehensible practices.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, consistency in the enforcement of the union's essential consumer law acquis contributes, and must contribute, to strengthening consumer confidence in the european digital single market. regulation 2006/2004 established a network of national law enforcement authorities to ensure the consistent cross-border enforcement of the main union consumer laws. the evaluation of that regulation, however, showed that the existing rules needed to be revised to respond to the challenges of the digital economy and the development of cross-border trade in the european union. effective coordination between the various competent authorities within that network is necessary, as well as effective coordination between other public authorities at member state level. the agreed text is the result of months of compromise between the commission's initial proposal and the positions of parliament and the council, and provides for modern, effective and efficient mechanisms. as the shadow rapporteur for the european people's party, i would like to applaud five key aspects of the text we are going to vote on. first, it strengthens the enforcement of consumer laws throughout the union, without affecting the competences of the member states in this area. second, it provides a common set of broad minimum powers for the competent authorities of the member states. thirdly, it increases the level of harmonisation which results in effective and efficient cooperation between the competent public authorities, covering the whole dimension of the single market. fourthly, it involves consumer and trader organisations in the mechanism. and fifth, it creates a common union procedure for widespread infringements at union level, ensuring that the commission has a strong coordinating role. contrary to what some claim, the exercise of the powers of this regulation must be proportionate and appropriate to the nature of the infringement and respect the provisions of national and union law, in particular the jurisdictional guarantees and fundamental rights. in conclusion, mr president, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mrs olga sehnalová, the shadow rapporteurs and the european commission on the result achieved which clearly benefits the rights of consumers in the union.
in malta there is no blatant attack on the 'rule of law' by the government as we see in hungary or poland. the problem in malta is the capture of the state, through members of the government, mps, supervisory authorities, magistrates, police, civil servants, by financial interests or intermediary services such as nexia bt. for this reason malta opposes the transparency on the actual beneficiaries of the trusts that this parliament proposes in the negotiations under the 5th anti-money laundering and terrorist financing directive. the problem is not confined to the current government: the commission has been working on the matter for a number of years, and it is not surprising that the commission has been able to take a number of initiatives to improve the situation in malta. the programme for the sale of maltese and european citizenship is at the top of these policies with greater risks to the security of malta and the european union. the bombing of the courageous journalist daphne caruana galizia is a shocking testimony to this. prime minister muscat keeps the chief of staff and the minister exposed by the panama papers and has yet to allow an independent investigation into allegations of an account linking his wife to the pilatos bank. how can you ensure that the maltese government properly safeguards the independence of the means of supervision and the depoliticization of the police and judicial authorities? it is not even intended to end the accumulation of political and state functions with the provision of financial, tax and business services! we can't fool ourselves. malta must rethink its development model and cease to be one of europe's centres for facilitating tax evasion and money laundering. it is up to the commission and the council not only to put pressure on malta to do so, but also to ensure, through european legislation, that these practices are outlawed and combated in malta, in the european union - and there are more tax havens like this in the european union - and globally.

mr president, the new anti-dumping methodology we are discussing here is a really important legislative initiative. the aim is to defend the european economy against imports from countries which compete on the basis of serious market distortions and unfair trade practices. with this more stringent method of controlling dumping, the european union ensures compliance with the rules of the world trade organisation, but above all it gains an instrument to defend the interests of its companies and workers, and with an advantage, to also value social dumping, because it takes account of social and environmental standards and distortions achieved through wage costs. as has been pointed out here, it is not the end of the road. we need to advocate a thorough modernization of our trade defence instruments. but one thing is certain, we are certainly better off with these anti-dumping instruments.

at the famous congress of tours in december 1920, the great french socialist léon blum stated, referring to the soviet leninists, who were then facing: for the first time in the entire history of socialism, you conceive of terrorism not only as a last-minute resort, not only as an extreme measure of public salvation in the face of bourgeois resistance, not even as a vital necessity for the revolution, but as a means of governance. that is, at the very beginning of the soviet process, there was a european left that opposed the soviet revolution. blum missed that convention, but he was right. the truth is that what was happening in the soviet union was the seizure of all power by a party vanguard which cancelled out any prospect of pluralist debate, prevented the affirmation of any model of democratic constitutionalism, prohibited the pursuit of critical reason and, given the class character it exalted, cancelled out its own universalist claim. a hundred years later, the balance we can take of the soviet revolution can only be a tragic balance.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, a better regulated global economy needs a multilateral tribunal capable of settling investment disputes fairly. the situation we have cannot continue. the anarchic multiplication of the most diverse arbitration systems in numerous bilateral and regional investment agreements, and the costs involved, do not guarantee a coherent resolution of disputes or the application of common rules. the european union is the world's largest exporter and importer of investment, so it is obviously in our interest to have this multilateral tribunal. and it is important that this debate should not be about the old model of private arbitration but about the new european proposal for a public arbitration system, as enshrined in the agreement with canada. and that is the commission's first task: to build confidence in the new model of public arbitration and to win the necessary support in the international community. the multilateral tribunal is only worthwhile if it is really new and not a solution from the past.
member of the commission. – mr president, this is indeed a historic night. so i have more speaking time, right? why are we doing this? mr fjellner is right that much of the debate in europe originated around the vattenfall issue. there was discussion before that, and also in other parts of the world there has been discussion of this. but that was probably the triggering event. it showed that there were issues related to transparency, procedures that were brought into question, and there were certainly questions related to legitimacy. this is a debate that is ongoing around the world. that is why it takes a little time to get everything right, because we do not want to impose a system that is european: we want to listen to our colleagues from other regions as well. canada and the eu are the leading ones on this, but many other countries are interested. we are looking at setting up a group of friends who could start the process, and start looking into more details of this. if i was unclear in my introduction: all the member states support this, it is not a commission-driven idea, it has the support of the member states. and i believe, judging from various votes, that it has the support of the majority of this house as well. we do not have to re-invent the wheel. we could very well envisage incorporating this into an existing organisation. the united nations commission on international trade law (uncitral) is now discussing it, they agreed that the system needs to be reviewed, so discussions are taking place there. the international centre for settlement of investment disputes (icsid) is one possibility, although it handles only 60% of cases, so there is not full coverage, and unanimity is required to change the rules, so that could be a cumbersome process, but it is certainly not excluded. issues related to tax evasion are important, mr marias, but they will not be discussed in the multilateral investment court. the intra-eu bilateral investment treaties (bits) are to be abolished. they cannot be transferred in. coming back to another question, there is a process being discussed internally to review the energy charter as well, and we are looking into this. one of my colleague is responsible, and we could come back to report on how that is going. what would the new court look like? it will have to have independent judges and they will be appointed on a permanent basis; there will have to be two instances; and there will have to be some sort of secretariat – not a huge bureaucracy but a secretariat. there will be costs, depending on how many participate. for purposes of comparison, the appellate body in the world trade organisation (wto) costs eur 10 million a year, but that is just one figure we can use as a comparative. the aim would be for the least-developed countries to pay less, and then we will have to see how much everybody should contribute. that will also depend on whether we create something new, or plug into something existing – or, rather, something slightly reformed. responsible business conduct is really important. there are laws that companies need to respect in the various countries, and european companies need to respect european laws even if they are operating abroad. discussions on investors’ responsibilities are going on in various international forums. there is the human rights working group in the united nations, and we are following that. it is a little too early to see how it is going. it was a bit dramatic at the start and the scope was unclear but we are participating and i have high hopes that this could lead to something, either as part of the discussion or in parallel. this is an important discussion and it is a firm objective of the commission that affected communities should have the opportunity to make their views known in proceedings before the future multilateral investment court, so they will have a voice there as well. they could be able to intervene with third-party submissions or amicus curiae. this should be allowed in the proceedings, but exactly how it should work is something we will have to come back to. lots of work has yet to be done. we are still at a very early stage on this, we need a critical mass of countries before we can begin, and i will be looking forward to continue reporting to parliament on how these deliberations are going.
in writing. i want to make it very clear by this statement that i agree with this question to the european commission and that it seeks to obtain some fundamental clarifications on the objectives, legal framework and mode of operation of this multilateral tribunal for the settlement of investment disputes. but at the same time i must say that i am not averse to attempts to use this or other parts of the trade agreements which the european union concludes with third countries in a sometimes blind and demagogic attempt to call into question an entire foreign and international trade policy. i consider these actions, carried out in the name of an outdated protectionism, to be completely out of step with the role that the eu must play as a global actor in order to protect its citizens, defend its values and even combat the deregulation that is the result of the process of globalisation.
in writing. in recent years, the inclusion of investor-state dispute settlement (isds) in trade and investment agreements has become the subject of increased concern and public intervention. some of the problems identified stem from the eiti, which is based on the principles of arbitrage. these problems relate in particular to the fact that the sgei has little or no legitimacy, consistency and transparency and that there is no scope for review. the institutionalisation of this investment dispute settlement system in eu trade and investment agreements through the inclusion of the investment court system (ics) is a further step in the creation of a court above nation states and above the sovereignty of peoples. it serves the interests of multinational companies which should be subject to the national laws of the countries in which they operate, as do most people and small and mediumsized enterprises.
mr president, the so-called pillar of social rights, signed in gothenburg, presented as a new initiative to strengthen the social dimension of the european union, is not really anything new. an initiative that keeps intact the whole of the policies associated with economic governance, the european semester, economic and monetary union or the budget treaty. the same policies which have, in the last decade, promoted brutal attacks on labour and social rights and which can only be seen to be deepening with more exploitation and impoverishment. in the 20 points, there are no proposals for raising wages, combating uncertainty, effectively defending collective bargaining, or a better redistribution of wealth. we will continue to denounce the deception of this pillar and to defend the need to promote social development, the appreciation of wages, the right to work and work with rights, the promotion of free and quality public services and the universality of social responses, including a public and universal social security.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank the house very much for its efforts to solve this issue. as mr hökmark already said, the basic idea in this legislation is to make the market economy more healthy, to show where the risks lie and also to create a more solid rule base for the problems which may arise, as happens every once in a while in the market economy. but this piece of legislation does not overshoot the regulation. it just clarifies who is responsible, and for what. this will enable banks to build up buffers that can absorb losses when the bank fails, without jeopardising financial stability, and without burdening taxpayers. banks will issue debt under the new statutory category available in all eu member states. the debt will be ranked just below the most senior debt and other senior liabilities, for the purposes of resolution. it will remain part of the senior debt category, but as an unpreferred tier senior debt. clear, harmonised rules on the position of bondholders in the bank creditors’ hierarchy in insolvency resolution will facilitate the bail-in being supplied, by providing greater legal certainty and reducing the risk of legal challenges. this measure will give banks clarity so that they can build up buffers to absorb losses and protect taxpayers. it is also a key, and most cost-effective, way to comply with the global standard on total loss absorbing capacity, which requires global systemically important banks to build up a minimum level of buffers to be used in resolution. our ambition is to allow banks and investors to take full advantage of the opportunities of our single market to issue and buy debt, through harmonised solutions. once again i would like to thank parliament, especially mr roberto gualtieri and, of course, mr hökmark, as rapporteur, and all the shadow rapporteurs for this file. this was one important piece of the entire banking package. we still have a lot of work to do to finalise the whole banking package and we have to keep working together in a fast and comprehensive way.
the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. so we must not fail to point this out when we have the opportunity. the truth is that this legislative proposal, which revises the hierarchy of creditors in the event of a resolution, has achieved this near miracle: the commission has not only adopted it in an accelerated procedure but has also succeeded in obtaining a broad consensus, both in this parliament and in the dialogue we have established with the council and the commission. i would like to thank the rapporteur, my colleague gunnar hökmark, but also the shadow rapporteurs from the other political groups, for the excellent work we have had the opportunity to do together. this legislative proposal is important and necessary. it seeks to improve the rules of the so-called bail-in, the internal rescue of banks in danger of bankruptcy, which were created to protect taxpayers' money. these rules need to be clear. we've changed the hierarchy of creditors to create a new category of senior unsecured debt, which increases the ability of banks to absorb losses and achieve the necessary recapitalization. i should say that the socialist group is particularly pleased that the contractual maturity of the new class of senior unprivileged debt has been fixed at at least one year, that grandfathering has been established, that is to say that the new eligibility criteria can be applied to debt instruments previously issued. it has also become clear that debt issued by banks under the previous national legislation, but which meets the requirements now laid down, may occupy the same position in the hierarchy of creditors as the new debt category now created. finally, and very importantly, it has been clarified that nothing in this directive affects the possibility for member states to give added protection to deposits. i think therefore that we have a good solution here and we hope that it can be continued in the revision of the drb directive.
mr president, this directive harmonises the position of unsecured claims in the context of normal insolvency proceedings. this issue has become necessary in the framework of the banking union and the single resolution scheme which provides that all financial institutions, so-called systemic, can demonstrate a sufficient level of total loss absorbing capacity. this proposal has the merit of being consistent with the principles and objectives of the banking union to replace the logic of bail-out with the alternative logic of bail-in, i.e. to ensure a resolution without public funds it is necessary to safeguard that each institution can present on its balance sheet a set of high quality assets that ensure loss coverage and keep the bank operating at least in its vital basic functions. the problem is that the creation and operation of the banking union has not only failed to prevent the use of more public funds, but has also promoted the concentration of the sector, increasing the weight of institutions that are considered too big to fail. it is not by chance that the discussion of a backstop mechanism to give the whole system solidity is on the agenda. that is, everything contrary to the stated objectives. for our part, we continue to believe that only public control of the financial system can put banking at the service of the economy and not the other way around.
mr president, the european disability strategy should be considered as a relevant guiding document on the safeguards for disabled citizens. we note, however, the criticisms of disabled people's organisations and associations, also expressed in the report, of the limited scope of the strategy and its limited reach in the absence of funding for its implementation, and for not contributing to the objectives of employment, social inclusion and poverty reduction. moreover, these same organisations also state that the ratification of the united nations convention on the rights of persons with disabilities has so far had no effect on the measures adopted at european level. on the other hand, we reaffirm that strategies and intentions will be of little use if they are not accompanied by a break with the policies of the european union, which in recent years have been responsible for the austerity revenues that have had a dramatic impact on citizens with disabilities and which have promoted drastic cuts in social support, the attack on public services, the increase in unemployment, with the consequent increase in poverty.
mr president, commissioner, solidarity is today, in modern societies, a political imperative of democratic states based on the rule of law. it is not, as in the past, the expression of religious feelings motivated by individual beliefs, but rather a collective obligation of essentially ethical content. solidarity emerges from a principle of social co-responsibility, according to which, in a human community, we are all responsible for everyone else. it is no wonder that it has come to occupy a prominent place in the framework of the legislative initiatives of the european union itself. it is in this context that the european disability strategy must be framed. that a disabled person is, thanks to this circumstance, different is a fact! but the fact that she should never feel diminished in her status as a citizen implies a responsibility to all other citizens and, above all, to the public authorities. the commission has also been asked to draw up a report on the implementation of the directive on the protection of the environment and the protection of the health of workers. it is a legal and political obligation for this parliament to ensure that this body of rights has an effective normative content in the european union, but it is up to all citizens of the union to ensure that these rights are not reduced to the proclaiming ornaments of legislative preambles or the rhetorical flower of political speeches, but have practical meaning and utility for disabled citizens.
member of the commission. – mr president, i would like to finish by thanking you for today’s constructive discussion and for your valuable remarks. it shows the european parliament’s strong commitment to the implementation of the united nations convention. the objectives of our european disability strategy remain fully relevant and we are committed to complete its actions before 2020. by 2020 it is expected that one european citizen in five will experience a degree of disability. the challenge is not becoming smaller, all the more reason to start our reflection on europe’s approach to implement the un convention after 2020. in 2018 i will launch an external independent evaluation of the outcomes of the current strategy. at the same time, we will ensure a structured process to collect the views of stakeholders and citizens. your resolution is an important contribution to define and elaborate the post-2020 scenario and i count on your continued involvement.
in writing. disability issues are central to building fair, supportive and developed societies. the united nations convention on the rights of persons with disabilities sets out technical and policy-based measures that deserve our support. the european disability strategy should also be considered as a relevant guidance document for safeguarding the rights of citizens with disabilities. but if paper holds up, what is needed is to translate the good principles and guidelines inscribed there into the concrete reality of the lives of people with disabilities. and here, unfortunately, there is a lot that remains to be done, as organizations and associations of the disabled have been denouncing. the ratification of the united nations convention on the rights of persons with disabilities has not so far had any effect on the measures adopted at european level. the lack of funding for the implementation of the european strategy is noteworthy. the commission has also been asked to provide a report on the implementation of the new financial perspective. in addition, strategies and good intentions will be of little use if they are not accompanied by the necessary breaks with eu policies, which perpetuate the so-called austerity and have a dramatic impact on citizens with disabilities and their living conditions.
mr president, i welcome the development of this process which has made it possible to make the necessary adjustments in order to respond promptly to the realities of the mediterranean pastures and to the challenges of youth employment in agriculture and sharp price fluctuations. however, the milk package, with measures now extended to all sectors, has not prevented the latest price crisis in the milk sector, from which many farmers, those who persisted, are still recovering. measures such as the timely concertation of supply are difficult to implement by all. for example, in mountain areas or in the outermost regions, geographical constraints make it difficult to achieve a relevant trade volume compared to the other links in the value chain. the changes in insurance and risk stabilisation instruments are positive, but we cannot expect them to replace a strong public policy in agriculture, otherwise production will be abandoned and concentrated in only a few areas of the european territory.
mr president, the defence of agriculture is not a sectoral issue. it aims to ensure that all europeans have access to high-quality agricultural products at low prices. and so it's very important that we look at the value chain in a different way. and so it is very important that in this document we have strengthened producer organizations and the creation of trading organizations, ensuring that there are profit-sharing and loss-sharing mechanisms in place, so that when prices fall in the market everyone behaves in the same way, whether they fall or rise. i would also like to stress the importance of having created a new risk management instrument, namely income insurance, and of allowing member states to make financial contributions to these premiums. i hope that the commission will take these standards into account, since they are essential for the outermost regions, where it is also very important that we support generational renewal by giving more support to young farmers for new agricultural installations and by creating mechanisms for generational renewal and the transfer of knowledge.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur once again on the inclusive work she has done with the shadow rapporteurs and their teams. it seems to me that a good compromise has been reached and a balanced final result. the fight against illegal, unreported and unregulated fishing is a priority for all. given the different levels of implementation between member states, it was preferable to remove the so-called eligibility criteria for fishing outside union waters. this ensures that there is no double penalty, which would only apply to fishermen and vessels from countries that are already fully complying with the control regulations. the prevaricators would benefit. ensuring that international fisheries agreements and european fisheries are not used as a tool for money laundering and tax evasion is a priority. the recording of information on vessels fishing in international waters, what fishing opportunities are associated with them, who their owners are and, very importantly, who the final beneficiaries are, makes all the activity carried out more transparent. at a time when efforts are being made to combat tax evasion, i was surprised at the difficulty of securing this particular point. we must continue to fight iuu fishing and promote respect for the environment and local communities in third countries, and we must therefore encourage fisheries agreements originating in the european union. we cannot allow the inefficient and dormant european international agreements to continue. marine resources are a common good and promoting sustainable, balanced and transparent exploitation by the community fleet is a way of demonstrating to our international partners how committed we are to this. this document is another instrument that helps ensure food security and fish from sustainable sources, as well as combating iuu fishing and transnational organised crime in the fishing industry.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, tax havens harm the poorest, they burden taxpayers, they harm competition, especially small and mediumsized enterprises, they undermine and also damage economic growth and our social model. the journalists, we thank them, have joined various consortia, denounced and made visible a problem which, if it is to be effectively combated, also needs a new consortium, on a global scale, that of governments, but also requires from us what we can already do from the european union. it is not necessary to standardize the rates, but we can standardize the tax base, at least the concepts and definitions. we should have a common and strict definition of tax havens in the european union. the proposals we have are also simple: the existence of a tax identification number for the european union, the automatic exchange of information between tax authorities, the introduction of a consolidated common tax base, the control of resident companies and the penalisation of companies which do not comply with the legislation.

for the gue/ngl group as we said at the beginning, we do not follow the logic and objectives of this investment fund. we believe that what is needed at the moment is a comprehensive public investment plan for all member states with positive discrimination criteria for the most deprived regions. there is no shortage of resources, as can be seen from the ecb's policy of quantitative easing. this juncker plan, by its competitive nature, favours above all the wealthier regions, does not guarantee additionality and promotes the delivery of public infrastructure to public-private partnerships. but if we look at the results in portugal, we see that the very logic behind the plan is not being followed. what we have in the background are loans from the european investment bank which could have been granted without the juncker plan. for the most part, we do not support the extension of this plan. we do not support the idea of creating another instrument of constraint for the national states, an investment fund which decides in an undemocratic way which investments to make and in which country. we, for our part, continue to advocate a budget of solidarity, with public funds, but in which it is for the national governments to decide how investment should be carried out in order to meet the needs of the people and then drag in private investment, which we have nothing against.
madam president, the agreement reached in the trilogue on the revision of the juncker plan is a good agreement and i would therefore like to congratulate parliament's negotiating team and our two rapporteurs. with this new version we will have a longer, stronger and fairer investment plan. finally, the problem of insufficient geographical distribution of investments, which tend to favour the most powerful economies rather than promoting territorial cohesion, was recognised. it is true that the dynamism of the countries can minimise these constraints, as is shown by the case of portugal, which, thanks to the excellent work of the government and local authorities, is today one of the countries most supported by the juncker plan. but we need these new rules and these new instruments to improve the geographical distribution of this fund and so that it can become, as it should, another strategic investment in favour of economic convergence in europe.

madam president, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on the quality of her report. unfortunately, from year to year and from report to report, we find that human rights, instead of progressing, are in some cases suffering serious setbacks in various regions of the world. it would be unfair to ignore that some progress has been made and that much of it has been achieved with the direct or indirect involvement of the european union. but these are still timid and insufficient advances. war and terrorism, the situation of migrants and refugees, human trafficking, slavery, the criminalisation of fundamental freedoms, the persecution of minorities, the repression of human rights defenders, the criminalisation of abortion, the contempt for indigenous peoples, the disregard for the rights of women and children, among others, all present a very dangerous picture, because they are phenomena that are often interlinked. this scenario therefore requires the european union to live up to its values and responsibilities. the truth is that there are millions of people around the world whose human rights are violated from many angles and who look to the european union as their last hope for some protection, justice and redress. and the european union, for historical, philosophical and cultural reasons, must do everything in its power not to abandon these same people.
madam president, this is certainly the time for ambitious european solutions, but this is not the case for the joint declaration to be signed tomorrow by the three institutions. it lacks this kind of ambition – to start with, on the way to deliver the european social pillar, because we want to update social standards for all citizens, starting with young people, in all member states. for this, we need to have more powerful instruments to support convergence: real convergence, economic and social convergence. take the reform package for economic and monetary union: we welcome the fact that important movements will take place to introduce more democracy when it comes to the accountability of the european stability mechanism. but let me be clear: if you want to have upward convergence, reforms are needed, but these are not enough; we need to have a stronger priority for investment, and this investment priority remains too weak in the integrated fiscal compact, too weak in the instrument to support convergence, too weak even in the so-called instrument to protect investment. the only way to have stronger priority for investment is to have an upcoming community budget with new own resources supporting stronger future—oriented investment. we need to get these, and we count on the estonian presidency to deliver on this.
madam president, the withdrawal agreement between the united kingdom and the european union must not in any way affect the workers' rights and other social rights enjoyed by citizens of the member states of the european union in the united kingdom, including the right of residence, the right to non-discriminatory treatment, the right of access to public services, health and education, the portability of benefits and social security rights, the right to family reunification, the mutual recognition of academic and professional qualifications, among other examples, something that is not acquired at the current stage of the negotiations and is not explicit in the joint motion for a resolution. any future agreement between the european union and the united kingdom should take into account the interests and specificities of the different member states. moreover, this agreement should not, and cannot, take away the right of each member state to establish relations with the united kingdom in a wide range of fields based on the interests and mutual benefit of their peoples.
madam president, first of all i would like to express my strongest disapproval of the way in which president tusk has presented the refugee question to the council. i think it is unacceptable for a community of law which defends human rights, which is undoubtedly a reference to fundamental rights, to treat the refugee question in this way and therefore it is deplorable and must be put aside. we stand with the commission and president juncker on this matter. i would then like to congratulate michel barnier on the results he has achieved, on his commitment, on his determination, on the way he has defended the rights of european citizens, including all british citizens on the continent and also in the british isles. and finally, he had a message for the brexit minister, mr. david davis: once upon a time, even gentlemen's agreements were binding. this is the message i wanted to leave. even a gentlemen's agreement is binding, which will be negotiated between institutions such as the british government and the european commission and the european council!
the best course of action for the negotiations between the european union (eu) and the united kingdom (uk) would be that which could lead to the uk remaining in the eu. there is, however, a democratic mandate of the british electorate for the negotiation of withdrawal, which must be respected as long as this is the expressed will of that electorate or of those who represent it. in this context, the european parliament's joint resolution on the negotiating process for the uk's withdrawal from the eu affirms a sensible line of negotiation, defends the interests of the eu and the rights of its citizens in the withdrawal process and already sets out the broad lines of a framework for future cooperation that can minimise the damage of withdrawal and preserve a good relationship between peoples. the resolution under discussion is therefore a good compromise text, which reiterates the red lines for negotiation set by the european parliament, identifies points to be safeguarded in the negotiating process in the field of rights, the irish question and financial compensation and outlines the future potential framework for association between the eu and the uk as a country outside the union.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, social europe is back in european debates and political initiative, and the proclamation of the social pillar is indeed a very important moment to make sure that the roadmap for the future of the european union will be guided by this ambition to update our social dimension to cope with the new challenges. this is the level of ambition we should bear in mind. and we should have a second ambition, which is to make sure that all european citizens, wherever they live or work, in all member states, can count on a powerful upward social and economic convergence, because we are still dealing with many inequalities, and without stronger cohesion there is no future for the european project. now, after the proclamation, the time for delivery has come, and let me be precise on the expectations from the s&d group. we are now really focused on making sure that all people in employment, whatever the kind of job, can count on two very important things. one is a transparent and decent labour contract. this is particularly important for young people. the second thing is full access to social protection throughout the life cycle, and we believe these are two key pieces to building a welfare system for the 21st century. so we are now expecting the commission to table a legislative initiative on this, now that the social partners could not come to an agreement. but let me also say that we believe that updating social standards and social law is not enough. we need to redirect social policies and economic policies, and that is why the european semester is so important, because the european semester must become a powerful process to coordinate these policies for upward convergence. we appreciate the package presented by the commission. it moves in this direction, but let me tell you that the big test will come when member states are confronted with big macro-social imbalances and we need to correct the situation.
mr president, commissioner, the european social pillar is the historical expression of the european spirit, that is to say, of the ambition for progress, of the will to do more and better, in short, of the constant nonconformity to the achievements of life and history. the european spirit was born from the battle of salamis and the defeat of the dark. it is based on greek philosophy and hellenism, roman law, renaissance humanism, the enlightenment's illumination and the triumph of scientific rationalism over myth and superstition. this idiosyncrasy of europeans means that just 7% of the world's population produces over 20% of the world's wealth and spends about 50% of all social spending on the planet, allowing europe to have achieved, in peace, freedom and democracy, levels of collective well-being unmatched anywhere else or at any time in history. it is this european spirit which will very soon enable us, through the robotization of the economy, to free man from the oppressive and alienating aspects of work, and to make him a voluntary contributor to the economic, scientific and cultural development of mankind.
mr president, the 20 principles listed in the so-called social pillar are a hoax, an attempt to whitewash the policies of the european union and to spread the idea of compatibility between labour and social rights and policies which objectively limit or deny them. social justice is inseparable from economic policy. a truly social europe is not compatible with the policies of the euro, banking union, economic governance, the european semester, militarisation, securitisation, the constant attack on the sovereignty and development of peoples. the pillar is an exercise in intentions that are countered by those policies, and empty of concrete measures, that promote the effective combating of the serious social problems that exist. a truly social europe is only possible if it is based on the uncompromising defence of work with rights, collective bargaining, real wage increases, a fair redistribution of wealth and tax justice, social protection supported by universal and quality public services, universal and free access to health and education. it is not compatible with the policies of the european union or the interests it protects.
the so-called 'social pillar' is more than a kind of social facade that you want to put on a structurally anti-social building. it does not dismiss the intention of levelling living and working conditions in europe below the level of the present. on the contrary, give him shelter. social justice is inseparable from economic policy. a truly social europe is not compatible with the policies of the euro, the banking union, economic governance, the european semester, militarisation and securitisation, the constant attack on the sovereignty and development of peoples. it is revealing that, at the same time as the 'social pillar' is being discussed, it is decided to internalize the so-called budget treaty in the eu legal order. this pillar is a show of intent opposed by those policies and a void of concrete measures that promote the effective fight against the serious social problems that exist. a truly social europe is only possible if we uncompromisingly defend work with rights, collective bargaining, real wage increases, a fair redistribution of wealth, tax justice, social protection backed by universal and quality public services, universal and free access to health and education. it is not compatible with the policies of the european union or the interests it protects.

mr president, commissioner, minister, throughout this year in this parliament we have had several debates on disasters which have taken on a great dimension because of the new context of climate change. it is a dimension and a reality that is new and therefore the answers must also be new. in all these discussions, after the diagnosis, we concluded that we needed to take action. improve and optimise response mechanisms at all levels and focus on union-wide cooperation. it is therefore very important, commissioner, that we are here today to see a concrete initiative that can provide immediate answers to the citizens. - i'm not going to. this is what the union needs. it is measures which translate into direct benefits for people, for their safety and for their quality of life. measures which mean a strengthening of european funds and resources and which are part of a common strategy. that said, commissioner, we cannot lose sight of the ambition to one day build a civil protection union, but that ambition must motivate us even more to implement the rescue and to take advantage of the increase in financial resources and available means. three ways to do that. in a top-down logic, to strengthen the common means, to streamline processes, to combine information and to share knowledge. support the strengthening of member states' means and indirectly create harmonisation and interoperability that will help improve national prevention and response systems. finally, by promoting cooperation and synergies between the means available, a practice which has already occurred on several occasions, but, as i said, with phenomena which are endemic, systemic and transnational, there is often no possible answer to all the needs. the amplification of disasters is not a theory. it is part of the daily life of people in general and europeans in particular. so, response amplification is also no longer a theory, and that's good news. let us put it into practice as soon as possible.
mr president, portugal is a tragic example of the need and importance of a competent european civil protection mechanism. our concerted and committed effort can save businesses, villages, forests, and most importantly, it can save lives. this year portugal has lost more than a hundred lives which, if this mechanism had worked as we wish and as we are discussing today, might perhaps have been saved. we must commit our resources to what matters most, and i stress again, what matters most in this europe which must be solidary, is lives, people. fires, earthquakes, floods, they will always be inevitable. the only difference will be in how we can predict them, how we can avoid them, and if they do happen, how we can face them and fight them. this will mean that it will even be possible to pool resources which are often scattered and not used for the benefit of all europeans in the future. do it then! i am sure that he will have the support of all members of the european parliament.
mr president, i would also like to welcome the commissioner and the minister for the council. i congratulate you on your proposal and on the work you have done. we all know the number of lives lost this year in the european union and in my country more than a hundred people have lost their lives as a result of fires. this shows that the solidarity of the european union must be expressed in concrete actions, and this new initiative is also a concrete expression of this solidarity. we must not, however, delude ourselves. it is true that it is essential to strengthen the european civil protection mechanism, but more important is the need to strengthen prevention. and that has to be the bet. and this is not the only fund that exists or will exist for solidarity, the structural funds are also for solidarity and must be used, first and foremost, for prevention. and that responsibility lies with the member states, and it is the member states that must take up and give substance to the dictum that prevention is better than cure. i would also like to conclude by saying that for new priorities we need new resources, additional resources, and i congratulate you on using the flexibility instrument in this proposal. i hope the council will also understand this simple fact that for new priorities, additional money, new resources, our proposal will always be that we should not accept reallocation and that we should use the margins and the flexibility instrument.
mr president, we agree with many of the considerations and recommendations contained in the report, but it is clear that the report under discussion today could and should have been more ambitious, particularly in terms of the need for defence and investment in sectors which already fall within the scope of low-carbon mobility, such as rail transport. while we understand the need for concrete measures to reduce emissions from the transport sector, we also understand that profound changes are needed in land use planning, regional and local development policies, distribution models, the construction, management and maintenance of public transport networks, infrastructures and services, public transport policies, road and highway planning and maintenance. this will only be possible if we break with the conditions and policies of the european union which make it possible, within the framework of the sovereignty of the states, to define powerful public investment policies associated with public control of strategic sectors such as transport or energy.
